\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{t})\|\hat{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|H_{t}))|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(H_{T}\in\cdot|\theta)\|\hat{P}(H_{T}\in\cdot)\right)\big|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\frac{1}{T}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta)}{\hat{P}(H_{T})}\bigg|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\frac{1}{T}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta)}{\frac{1}{|\Theta_{\epsilon}|}\cdot\mathbb{P}(H_{t}|\theta=\tilde{\nu}^{*})}\bigg|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\frac{\ln|\Theta_{\epsilon}|}{T}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta=\tilde{\nu}^{*},H_{t})\right)|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathfrak{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\Theta)}{T}+\epsilon,

where
(a)
and
(b)
follow from the chain rule of KL divergence and the definition of
\hat{P}
and
(c)
follows from the definition of metric entropy.
∎

We note that Theorem
21
and Theorem
17
very closely resemble each other. For any upper bound on the Bayes regret which we derive via Theorem
17
, a similar technique can be applied to bound the minimax regret via Theorem
21
. However, as mentioned above, the choice of confidence set will dramatically impact the resulting bounds in the frequentist framework. Our observation is that for a problem such as learning with deep neural networks, naive specification of the confidence set will result in vacuous worst-case sample complexity bounds. Constructing confidence sets which better reflect the problems we may encounter in the real world and are amenable to analysis is a significant outstanding challenge. However, Theorem
21
serves as a bridge which relates the results of this monograph to those of the frequentist framework.

5.3.3
Minimax Lower Bounds

The lower bound for Bayesian regret via the rate-distortion function in Theorem
17
, in tandem with the corresponding rate-distortion upper bounds, provides a tight characterization of the Bayesian error. Tight characterizations of this sort are not unforeseen as
Yang and Barron [1999]
demonstrate similar tight characterizations of the minimax error in terms of metric and packing entropy. Theorem
1
of
[
Yang and Barron, 1999
]
establishes a lower bound on the minimax regret which we detail here for completeness. The result holds for loss functions which obey a triangle inequality. It is well known that the square-root KL divergence does not generally exhibit a triangle inequality, however, if the set of densities corresponding to the probability measures
\{\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta=\nu,X_{t}):\nu\in\Theta\}
have uniformly bounded logarithms, then the square root KL divergence satisfies the following
local
triangle inequality:

Lemma 22
.

(local triangle inequality)

If there exists
M<\infty
such that

\sup_{P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}}\|\ln dP^{\prime}\|_{\infty}<M,

where
dP^{\prime}
denotes the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
P^{\prime}
w.r.t the Lebesgue measure, then there exists
A\in(0,1],\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any probability measures
P(\cdot),P^{\prime}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}
and any probability measure
\tilde{P}(\cdot)
defined on the same
\sigma
algebra, if

\max\left\{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P(\cdot)\|\tilde{P}(\cdot)\right),{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{\prime}(\cdot)\|\tilde{P}(\cdot)\right)\right\}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{2},

then

\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P(\cdot)\|\tilde{P}(\cdot))}+\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{\prime}(\cdot)\|\tilde{P}(\cdot))}\geq A\cdot\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P(\cdot)\|P^{\prime}(\cdot))}.

Before we provide the lower bound, we first introduce another important quantity for characterizing the complexity of a set
\Theta
.

Definition 23
.

(packing entropy)

Let
\epsilon\geq 0
,
\Theta
be a set, and
\rho:\Theta\times\Theta\to\Re_{+}
a distortion function. The packing entropy of set
\Theta
at tolerance
\epsilon
is:

\sup_{\tilde{\Theta}\in\Theta_{\epsilon}}\ \ln|\tilde{\Theta}|

where

\Theta_{\epsilon}=\left\{\tilde{\Theta}\subseteq\Theta:\ \inf_{\tilde{\nu}\in\tilde{\Theta}}\inf_{\nu\neq\tilde{\nu}\in\tilde{\Theta}}\rho(\nu,\tilde{\nu})\geq\epsilon\right\}.

While the metric entropy is the logarithm of the minimum size quantization necessary to
cover

\Theta
up to tolerance
\epsilon
, the packing entropy is the logarithm of the maximum size quantization which
packs

\Theta
with tolerance
\epsilon
. This packing ensures that no two elements of the quantization are within
\epsilon
distortion of one another. In order to relate packing entropy to
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
, we use the following distortion:

\rho(\nu,\tilde{\nu})=\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta=\nu,X_{0})\ \|\ \mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta=\tilde{\nu},X_{0})\right)}\right].

We use the notation
\mathfrak{M}_{\epsilon}(\Theta)
to denote the packing entropy:

\mathfrak{M}_{\epsilon}(\Theta)=\sup_{\tilde{\Theta}\in\Theta_{\epsilon}}\ \ln|\tilde{\Theta}|,

where

\Theta_{\epsilon}=\left\{\tilde{\Theta}\subset\Theta:\inf_{\tilde{\nu}\in\tilde{\Theta}}\inf_{\nu\neq\tilde{\nu}\in\tilde{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta=\tilde{\nu},X_{0})\right)}|\theta=\nu\right]\geq\epsilon\right\}.

With this requisite notation in place, we present the minimax lower bound (Theorem 1 of
[
Yang and Barron, 1999
]
).

Theorem 24
.

(minimax lower bound)

Let
\Theta
be a set. For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, let
\epsilon_{t}>0
satisfy
\epsilon_{t}=\mathfrak{H}_{\epsilon,t}(\Theta)/t
and
\underline{\epsilon}_{t}
satisfy
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}(\Theta)=4t\epsilon_{t}+2\ln 2
. If
\underline{\epsilon}_{T}<2\epsilon_{0}
then

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\geq\frac{A^{2}}{8}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}^{2},

where
A,\epsilon_{0}
are the constants from Lemma
22
.

Proof.

Let
\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}
be the
\underline{\epsilon}_{T}
-packing which attains
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}(\Theta)
. Let

\tilde{\theta}=\argmin_{\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}}\ \mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}\right)}|\theta=\theta^{\prime}\right].

Therefore,
\tilde{\theta}
is the element in the packing
\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}
with minimum expected square root KL divergence from the posterior distribution
\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1})
. Going forward, we assume that
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)={\rm Uniform}(\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}})
.

For brevity, we let

\displaystyle d(\theta,P_{T})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))}|\theta]

\displaystyle d(\tilde{\theta},P_{T})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\sqrt{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))}|\theta=\tilde{\theta}].

Then,

\displaystyle\theta\neq\tilde{\theta}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\implies}d(\theta,P_{T})+d(\tilde{\theta},P_{T})\geq A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}{\rm\ if\ }\max\left\{d(\theta,P_{T}),d(\tilde{\theta},P_{T})\right\}<\frac{A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies d(\theta,P_{T})+d(\tilde{\theta},P_{T})\geq A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}{\rm\ if\ }d(\theta,P_{T})<\frac{A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies 2d(\theta,P_{T})\geq A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}{\rm\ if\ }d(\theta,P_{T})<\frac{A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies d(\theta,P_{T})\geq\frac{A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}{2}{\rm\ if\ }d(\theta,P_{T})<\frac{A\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies d(\theta,P_{T})\geq\frac{A\epsilon_{T}}{2},

where
(a)
follows from the local triangle inequality, the fact that
\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}
is an
\underline{\epsilon}_{T}
packing, and
\theta\neq\tilde{\theta}
.

Then,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{T}

\displaystyle=\inf_{P}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{t})\|P(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|H_{t}))|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{P}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|P(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\inf_{P}\sup_{\nu\in\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|P(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))|\theta=\nu\right]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{P}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|P(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1}))\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{A}{2}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}\right)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left({\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{T-1})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{T}\in\cdot|H_{T-1})\right)\geq\left(\frac{A}{2}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\left(\frac{A}{2}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}\right)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}\left(\frac{A}{2}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}\right)^{2}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{I}(\theta;H_{T})+\ln 2}{\mathbb{H}(\theta)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\geq}\left(\frac{A}{2}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}\right)^{2}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\mathfrak{H}_{\epsilon_{T},T}(\Theta)+T\epsilon_{T}+\ln 2}{\mathbb{H}(\theta)}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{A^{2}}{8}\underline{\epsilon}_{T}^{2},

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
\Theta_{\underline{\epsilon}_{T}}\subseteq\Theta
,
(b)
follows from the above implication,
(c)
follows from Fano’s inequality,
(d)
follows from the fact that expected error lower bounds minimax error and Theorem
21
.
∎

We note that
Yang and Barron [1999]
provide techniques which relax some of the conditions in Theorem
24
pertaining to the local triangle inequality by instead constructing covers/packings according to the Hellinger distance (Corollary 3).

As we alluded to above, we observe that for problem settings which involve complex confidence sets
\Theta
, the result of bounding minimax error via Theorem
21
may be substantially worse than the result of bounding Bayesian error via Theorem
17
. Much as the entropy of a discrete random variable may be much smaller than the logarithm of the number of possible realizations, the Bayesian error can be much smaller than the minimax error when the confidence set
\Theta
is not chosen judiciously. In this work, we elect to study the Bayesian error as it appears that good sample complexity results are feasible even under
simple
choices of the prior distribution. Analyses of similar flavor may extend to the frequentist setting if suitable effort is taken in designing the confidence set.

Summary

•

(optimal error equals total information)
For horizon
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, the error of the optimal algorithm is denoted as
\mathcal{L}_{T}
and is

\mathcal{L}_{T}=\frac{\mathbb{I}(\theta;H_{T})}{T}.

•

For all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\leq\frac{\mathbb{H}(\theta)}{T}.

Therefore, for a discrete random variable
\theta
with finite entropy,
\mathcal{L}_{T}=O(1/T)
.

•

The extension of this result to continuous random variables can be made via rate-distortion theory.

•

Let
\tilde{\Theta}
be the set of random variables
\tilde{\theta}
such that
\tilde{\theta}\perp Y_{t+1}|(\theta,H_{t})
.

•

For tolerance
\epsilon
and horizon
T
, we define the
T
-horizon rate-distortion function as

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)=\inf_{\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon,T}}\ \mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta}),

where

\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon,T}=\left\{\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}:\ \underbrace{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\ \|\ \tilde{P}_{t}\right)\right]\leq\epsilon}_{\rm distortion\ threshold}\ \right\}.

•

(
T
-horizon rate-distortion error bounds)

For all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\sup_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \min\left\{\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)}{T},\epsilon\right\}\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon.

6
Learning from iid Data

In this section, we restrict our attention to the analysis of learning from data that is independently and identically distributed (iid) conditioned on
\theta
. Formally, we assume that
\{(X_{t},Y_{t+1})\}_{t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}
is iid conditioned on
\theta
. This specializes the framework introduced in previous sections, and therefore, our results from those sections hold under this iid assumption. In this section, we will derive stronger theoretical results under the iid assumption. In particular, we establish results for four specific data generating processes arising from
1)
linear regression,
2)
logistic regression,
3)
finite-width multi-layer perceptrons,
4)
infinite-width multi-layer perceptrons. We hope that this suite of examples provides the reader with enough intuition and tools to analyze their own problems of interest.

6.1
Theoretical Results Tailored for iid Data

Theorem
17
provides upper and lower bounds for the optimal error at horizon
T
via the
T
-horizon rate-distortion function
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}
. Under the iid assumption, we can simplify analysis by deriving bounds based on the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,0}
. Going forward, we will write
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}
as shorthand for
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,0}
. Hence,

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)=\inf_{\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}}\mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta}),

where

\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}=\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon,0}=\{\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}:\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})]\leq\epsilon\}.

The aforementioned bounds on
\mathcal{L}_{T}
in terms of
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)
derive from two lemmas, which we now establish. The first states that, under the iid assumption, error is monotonically non-increasing with time. Recall that
\hat{P}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|H_{t})
is our prediction, while
P^{*}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,H_{t})
is the gold standard that could be produced by a clairvoyant.

Lemma 25
.

(monotonicity of error)

If
((X_{t},Y_{t+1}):t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is an iid stochastic process when conditioned on
\theta
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t+1}\|\hat{P}_{t+1})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{P}_{t})\right].

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t+1}\|\hat{P}_{t+1})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(\theta;Y_{t+2}|H_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|H_{t+1})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|\theta,H_{t+1})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|H_{t+1})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|\theta,H_{t-1},Y_{t},X_{t+1})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|H_{t-1},Y_{t},X_{t+1})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{t+2}|\theta,H_{t-1},Y_{t},X_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+2};\theta|H_{t-1},Y_{t},X_{t+1})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|H_{t-1},Y_{t},X_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|H_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{P}_{t})\right],

where
(a)
follows since
Y_{t+2}\perp(X_{t},Y_{t+1})|(\theta,X_{t+1})
,
(b)
follows from the fact that conditioning reduces differential entropy, and
(c)
follows from the fact that
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
and
(X_{t+1},Y_{t+2})
are identically distributed conditioned on
(H_{t-1},Y_{t})
.
∎

This result is intuitive. Since
H_{t+1}
provides more information than
H_{t}
, the prediction made at time
t+1
ought to be better.

A second lemma bounds the
T
-horizon distortion via the
0
-horizon distortion. Recall that
\tilde{P}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},H_{t})
is the prediction based on an approximation
\tilde{\theta}
to
\theta
.

Lemma 26
.

(
0
-horizon distortion upper bounds
T
-horizon distortion)

If
((X_{t},Y_{t+1}):t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is iid conditioned on
\theta
and, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
Y_{t+1}\perp\tilde{\theta}|(\theta,X_{t})
then, for all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\underbrace{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]}_{T-{\rm horizon\ distortion}}\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]}_{0-{\rm horizon\ distortion}}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\theta|\tilde{\theta})}{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(X_{t},Y_{t+1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},H_{t-1},Y_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},H_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right],

where
(a)
follows from the assumption that
X_{t}\perp\theta
and
(b)
follows from the same proof technique as in Lemma
25
.
∎

These two results lead to simpler upper and lower error bounds than those presented in Theorem
17
. In particular, under the iid assumption, for any
T
, we can upper and lower bound
\mathcal{L}_{T}
via the
0
-horizon instead of
T
-horizon rate-distortion function. The
0
-horizon rate-distortion function is simpler to analyze, and this will allow us to establish tighter bounds later for specific data generating processes.

Theorem 27
.

(0-horizon rate-distortion error bound for iid data)

If
((X_{t},Y_{t+1}):t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is iid conditioned on
\theta
, then, for all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},

\sup_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \min\left\{\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T},\epsilon\right\}\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon.

Proof.

We begin with the lower bound. Fix
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
. Let
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{H}_{T-2},\tilde{Y}_{T-1})
be independent from but distributed identically with
(H_{T-2},Y_{T-1})
when conditioned on
\theta
.

Fix
\epsilon\geq 0
. If
\mathcal{L}_{T}<\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)/T
then

\mathcal{L}_{T}<\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)/T\ \implies\ \mathbb{I}(H_{T};\theta)<\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)\ \implies\ \mathbb{I}(\tilde{\theta};\theta)<\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta).

Since the rate of
\tilde{\theta}
is
lower
than the rate-distortion function
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)
,
\tilde{\theta}\notin\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}=\{\theta\in\tilde{\Theta}:\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})]\leq\epsilon\}
. As a result,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\theta)}{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|H_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}\mathbb{I}(Y_{T};\theta|H_{T-1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{T};\theta|\tilde{\theta},X_{T-1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{T-1}\|\tilde{P}_{T-1})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{>}\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
15
,
(b)
follows from the chain rule of mutual information,
(c)
follows from Lemma
25
,
(d)
follows from the iid assumption, and
(e)
follows from the fact that
\tilde{\theta}\notin\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
. Therefore,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\geq\min\{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)/T,\ \epsilon\}.

Since this holds for any
\epsilon\geq 0
, the lower bound result follows.

We next establish the upper bound. For all
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\theta)}{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|H_{t})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\ \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta,\tilde{\theta}|H_{t})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\ \frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\tilde{\theta}|H_{t})+\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},H_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\ \frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\tilde{\theta})}{T}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\inf_{\tilde{\theta}}\ \frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\tilde{\theta})}{T}+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from our regularity assumption and
(b)
follows from Lemma
26
.
∎

In the following four sections we will apply this result to specific data generating processes. We begin with linear regression.

6.2
Linear Regression

Figure 3
:
We depict our linear regression data generating process above. It consists of an input vector
X
of dimension
d
, an unknown weight vector
\theta
of dimension
d
, and a final output
Y
which is the sum of
\theta^{\top}X
and independent Gaussian noise
W
.

6.2.1
Data Generating Process

In linear regression, the variable that we are interested in estimating is a random vector
\theta\in\Re^{d}
. In our Bayesian framework, a known prior distribution
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)
expresses uncertainty about
\theta
. We assume that
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
. For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, inputs and outputs are generated according to a random vector
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

Y_{t+1}=\theta^{\top}X_{t}+W_{t+1},

where
W_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for known variance
\sigma^{2}
. It is straightforward to extend results of this section to Gaussian input and prior distributions with different means and covariance matrices and even distributions that are not Gaussian. We study our specific Gaussian case to reduce clutter without compromising insight.

6.2.2
Preliminary Results

In this section, we establish preliminary results that will allow us to streamline our error analysis using the tools established in Section
5
. These results characterize the rate-distortion function for our linear regression model.

Our first result pertains to levels of error that require no information. In particular, because no information is required to achieve the error delivered by an uninformed prediction, there should be a threshold
\overline{\epsilon}
such that
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)=0
for all
\epsilon\geq\overline{\epsilon}
. This result establishes the threshold.

Lemma 28
.

(linear regression 0 rate-distortion threshold)

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, if for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the linear regression process and
\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{2}\ln(1+1/\sigma^{2})
, then for all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)=0.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\theta}=\emptyset
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\theta,\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|X_{0})-\mathbf{h}(W)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\sigma^{2}+\text{Var}[\theta^{\top}X|X]\right)\right)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\sigma^{2}+\frac{\|X\|^{2}_{2}}{d}\right)\right)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\sigma^{2}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\|X\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{d}\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
,
(b)
follows from the fact that
\theta
has covariance matrix
I_{d}/d
, and
(c)
follows from Jensen’s inequality. The above establishes that
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
. The result follows from the fact that
\mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta})=0
.
∎

The preceding result addresses the trivial case where error is so large that no information is required. Our next result addresses all levels of error.

Lemma 29
.

(linear regression
0
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound)

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,
\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, and
\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the linear regression process, then for all
T
,

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)\leq\left(\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}(e^{2\epsilon}-1)}\right)\right)_{+}.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
. Let
\tilde{\theta}=\theta+V
, where
V\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\delta^{2}I_{d}/d)
for
\delta^{2}=\frac{\sigma^{2}(e^{2\epsilon}-1)}{1-\sigma^{2}(e^{2\epsilon}-1)}
and
V\perp\theta
. Note that
\delta^{2}\geq 0
for all
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\right)
. We begin by showing that
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\theta,\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(W_{1}+\theta^{\top}X|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})-\mathbf{h}(W_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}\left(W_{1}+\theta^{\top}X_{0}-\frac{\tilde{\theta}^{\top}X_{0}}{1+\delta^{2}}\bigg|\tilde{\theta},X_{0}\right)-\mathbf{h}(W_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}\left(W_{1}+\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{1+\delta^{2}}\theta+\frac{1}{1+\delta^{2}}V\right)^{\top}X_{0}\Big|\tilde{\theta},X_{0}\right)-\mathbf{h}(W_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{h}\left(W_{1}+\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{1+\delta^{2}}\theta+\frac{1}{1+\delta^{2}}V\right)^{\top}X_{0}\Big|X_{0}\right)-\mathbf{h}(W_{1})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\sigma^{2}+\left(\frac{\delta^{4}}{d(1+\delta^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{d(1+\delta^{2})^{2}}\right)\|X_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\right)\right)\right]-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\delta^{2}\|X_{0}\|^{2}_{2}}{d(1+\delta^{2})\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\delta^{2}}{(1+\delta^{2})\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(e^{2\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
and
(b)
follows from Jensen’s inequality. Next, we upper bound the rate of
\tilde{\theta}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta})-\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta}|\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta})-\mathbf{h}(V)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{\delta^{2}+1}{d}\right)\right)-\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{d}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(e^{2\epsilon}-1\right)}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
. The result follows from Lemma
28
and the fact that
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
for all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\epsilon<1/2\ln(1+1/\sigma^{2})
.
∎

Proofs of our previous two results did not rely on the assumption that
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
but only on the fact that the elements of
\theta
are independent with variances that sum to
1
. To establish a
lower bound
on the rate-distortion function, we rely on Gaussianity.

Lemma 30
.

(linear regression
0
-horizon rate-distortion lower bound)

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
s.t.
d>2
,
\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, and
\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the linear regression process, then

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)\ \geq\ \left(\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1}{(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})\epsilon}\right)\right)_{+}.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
,
\epsilon\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
. Then,

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};\theta|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|X_{0}\|^{2}_{2}}{2(4\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+d\sigma^{2})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\frac{2(4\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+d\sigma^{2})}{\|X_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{8+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{\|X_{0}\|^{2}_{2}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[8+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{\|X_{0}\|^{2}_{2}}\right]}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\frac{1}{8+\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{d-2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
76
,
(c)
follows from Jensen’s inequality, and
(d)
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[1/\chi^{2}(d)]=1/(d-2)
.

Since the above condition is an implication that holds for arbitrary
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon}
, minimizing the rate
\mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta})
over the set of proxies that satisfy

\mathbb{E}[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}]\leq(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})\epsilon
will provide a lower bound. However, this is simply the rate-distortion problem for a multivariate source under squared error distortion. For this problem there exists a well known lower bound (Theorem 10.3.3 of
[
Cover and Thomas, 2006
]
). The lower bound follows as a result.

∎

It is worth noting that this result extends to sub-Gaussian
\theta
, as established in Appendix
A
.

6.2.3
Main Result

The rate-distortion bounds which we established in the previous section allow us to directly apply Theorem
27
to arrive at error bounds.

Theorem 31
.

(linear regression error bounds)

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,
\sigma^{2}\geq 0
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the linear regression processes, then for all
T
,

\frac{d}{2T}W\left(\frac{2T}{d(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})}\right)\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \left(\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\right)_{+}+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right),

where
W
is the Lambert W function.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\left(d\ln\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}(e^{2\epsilon}-1)}\right)\right)_{+}}{2T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{\left(d\ln\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right)^{T}-1\right)}\right)\right)_{+}}{2T}+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\right)_{+}+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
27
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
29
, and
(c)
follows from setting
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \min\left\{\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T},\epsilon\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \min\left\{\left(\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{1}{(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})\epsilon}\right)\right)_{+},\ \epsilon\right\}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\text{ s.t. }\left(\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{1}{(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})\epsilon}\right)\right)_{+}=\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{e^{-W\left(\frac{8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\right)}}{\frac{2T}{d(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})}}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\frac{e^{-\ln\left(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2}\right)}}{\frac{2T}{d(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2T},

where in
(a)
,
W
denotes the Lambert
W
function and
(b)
follows from the fact that for all
x\geq e
,
W(x)\leq\ln(2x)
.
∎

Notably, this bound is consistent with classical results in statistics which dictate that mean squared error grows linearly in the problem dimension
d
and decays linearly in the number of samples observed
T
. In the following section, we will observe that qualitatively similar results also hold for logistic regression.

6.3
Logistic Regression

Figure 4
:
We depict our logistic regression data generating process above. It consists of an input vector
X
of dimension
d
, an unknown weight vector
\theta
of dimension
d
, and a final binary output
Y\in\{0,1\}
.
Y
is
sampled
according to probabilities generated by the sigmoid function applied to
\theta^{\top}X
.

6.3.1
Data Generating Process

We next study logistic regression to demonstrate the application of our tools to
classification
. Just as in linear regression, in logistic regression, the variable that we are interested in estimating is a random vector
\theta\in\Re^{d}
. We assume that
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
. For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, inputs and outputs are generated according to a random vector
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

Y_{t+1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ w.p. }\frac{1}{1+e^{-\theta^{\top}X_{t}}},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Our results can be generalized beyond these prior and input distributions.

6.3.2
Preliminary Results

We begin with the following result, which upper bounds the binary KL divergence of a sigmoidal output via the squared difference between the logits.

Lemma 32
.

(squared error upper bounds binary KL divergence)

For all
x,y\in\Re
,

\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}\ln\frac{1+e^{-y}}{1+e^{-x}}+\frac{1}{1+e^{x}}\ln\frac{1+e^{y}}{1+e^{x}}\leq\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{8}.

In contrast to a linear model with aleatoric randomness in the form of Gaussian noise, the logistic model exhibits aleatoric randomness via a Bernoulli sample. This will complicate calculation of the
0
-horizon distortion
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})]
, since the prediction
\tilde{P}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})
is not Gaussian for natural choices of
\tilde{\theta}
. To circumvent this issue, we provide a general upper bound for the
0
-horizon distortion function via the distortion of a prediction under a change of measure:
\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{t})
. Recall that the dependence of this prediction on
\tilde{\theta}
and
X_{t}
is expressed by a function
f
such that, for all
(\tilde{\nu},x)\in\tilde{\Theta}\times\mathcal{X}
,
f(x,\tilde{\nu})=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta=\tilde{\nu},X_{t}=x)
. The distortion incurred by
\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{t}
, which does not depend on
t
, is in general much simpler to upper bound for concrete problem instances.

Lemma 33
.

(
\tilde{P}_{0}
achieves lower error than the change of measure prediction)

If
\tilde{\theta}:\Omega\mapsto\tilde{\Theta}
is a random variable such that for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
Y_{t+1}\perp\tilde{\theta}|\theta,H_{t}
and
\tilde{\Theta}\subseteq\Theta
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{0})\right].

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\tilde{P}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}\bigg|\tilde{\theta},X_{0}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\theta,X_{0})}{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\theta,X_{0})}{\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{0})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{0})\right]

where
(a)
follows from the fact that KL divergence is non-negative.
∎

Using Lemmas
32
and
33
, we establish the following upper bound on the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function for logistic regression.

Lemma 34
.

(logistic regression
0
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound)

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the logistic regression process then

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)\ \leq\ \frac{d}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right).

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\theta}=\theta+Z
where
Z\perp\theta
and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,8\epsilon I/d)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\ \|\ \tilde{P}^{\prime}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta,X_{0})\ \|\ \mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta},X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\ln\left(\frac{1+e^{-\tilde{\theta}^{\top}X_{0}}}{1+e^{-\theta^{\top}X_{0}}}\right)}{1+e^{-\theta^{\top}X_{0}}}+\frac{\ln\left(\frac{1+e^{\tilde{\theta}^{\top}X_{0}}}{1+e^{\theta^{\top}X_{0}}}\right)}{1+e^{\theta^{\top}X_{0}}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta^{\top}X_{0}-\tilde{\theta}^{\top}X_{0}\right)^{2}\right]}{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{8}

\displaystyle=\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
33
and
(b)
follows from Lemma
32
.

We now upper bound the rate.

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\theta;\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta})-\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta}|\theta)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{1}{d}+\frac{8\epsilon}{d}\right)\right)-\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\frac{8\epsilon}{d}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
. The result follows.
∎

6.3.3
Main Result

With the
0
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound in place, we establish the following upper bound on the optimal horizon
T
error.

Theorem 35
.

(logistic regression estimation error upper bound)
For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated according to the logistic regression process then, for all
T,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{d}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{4d}\right)\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\frac{d}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right)}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{4d}\right)+\frac{d}{2T},

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
27
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
34
, and
(c)
follows from setting
\epsilon=\frac{d}{2T}
.
∎

Just as with linear regression, this error bound is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d/T)
. In the next two sections, we consider much more complex data generating processes, which are associated with multi-layer perceptrons.

6.4
Finite-Width Multi-Layer Perceptrons

Figure 5
:
We depict our finite-width multi-layer perceptron data generating process above. It consists of input dimension
d
, width
N
, depth
L
, with output dimension
1
, and ReLU activation units. We denote the weights at layer
\ell
by
A^{(\ell)}
and the output of layer
\ell
by
U^{(\ell)}
. We assume that the final output
Y
is the sum of the final network output
U^{(L)}
and independent Gaussian noise
Z
.

6.4.1
Data Generating Process

For a finite-width multi-layer perceptron (MLP),
\theta
consists of random matrices
(A^{(1)},\ldots,A^{(L)})
, which represent the weights that parameterize each of the
L
layers. For simplicity, we assume that the width of every hidden layer is identical and equal to a positive integer
N
and that the MLP has input dimension
d
and output dimension
1
. In our Bayesian framework, a known prior distribution
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)
expresses uncertainty over the MLP weights. We assume the following prior distribution:

\mathbb{P}(A^{(\ell)})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{I_{N\cdot d}}{d})&\text{ if }\ell=1,\\
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{I_{N\cdot N}}{N})&\text{ if }2\leq\ell\leq L-1,\\
\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{I_{N}}{N})&\text{ if }\ell=L.\end{cases}

We further make the assumption that the weights
across
layers are independent. These Gaussianity assumptions are not required to prove our results, though the specified covariance structure is. We make the Gaussianity assumptions only to simplify the proofs.

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, inputs and outputs are generated according to a random vector
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

\displaystyle U^{(0)}_{t}

\displaystyle=X_{t}

\displaystyle U^{(\ell+1)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\text{ReLU}\left(A^{(\ell+1)}U^{(\ell)}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle Y_{t+1}

\displaystyle=U^{(L)}_{t}+W_{t+1},

where
W_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for known variance
\sigma^{2}
.

6.4.2
Preliminary Results

In this section, we cover several lemmas which facilitate error analysis for MLPs. Lemma
39
establishes an upper bound on the horizon
0
distortion function in the finite-width MLP setting. This result allows us to easily derive upper bounds for the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function in Theorem
40
. We begin with the following three Lemmas (
36
,
37
,
38
) which facilitate the derivation of distortion upper bound in Lemma
39
. We begin with an initial result which decomposes distortion into a sum of simpler terms.

We first introduce some requisite notation to necessary to understand the following result. We restrict our attention to
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{A}^{1},\tilde{A}^{2},...,\tilde{A}^{(L)})
which are independent from each other. Under this assumption, we abbreviate the prediction based on an approximation of the first
\ell
layers
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell+1,L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell)},H_{t})
as
\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{t}
. The following result expresses the
0
-horizon distortion as the sum of distortions between consecutive approximate predictions across all layers.

Lemma 36
.

(
0
-horizon distortion decomposition)

If
(X_{0},Y_{1})
are generated by the finite-width MLP process and
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{L})
are independent random variables such that,
Y_{1}\perp\tilde{A}^{(1:L)}|A^{(1:L)},X_{0}
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]\ =\ \sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right].

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(1:L)}|\tilde{A}^{(1:L)},X_{0})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:L)},X_{0})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell)},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell:L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell-1)},X_{0})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell)},X_{0}))\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right],

where
(a)
follows from the chain rule and
(b)
follows from conditional independence assumptions.
∎

With this decomposition in mind, we can derive a suitable upper bound for the total distortion by bounding each
individual
term of the decomposition. The following two results establish upper bounds for the individual terms.

Lemma 37
.

If
(X_{0},Y_{1})
are generated by the finite-width MLP process and
\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{L}
are independent random variables such that,
Y_{1}\perp\tilde{A}^{(1:L)}|A^{(1:L)},X_{0}
, then for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U^{(\ell)}_{0}))\right].

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell)},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1},X_{0},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell-1)};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})-\mathbb{I}(X_{0},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell-1)};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1},X_{0},\tilde{A}^{(\ell-1:1)};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})+\mathbb{I}(X_{0},\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell-1)};A^{(\ell)}|Y_{1},A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})+\mathbb{I}(X_{0},A^{(1:\ell-1)};A^{(\ell)}|Y_{1},A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1},X_{0},A^{(1:\ell-1)};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|X_{0},A^{(1:\ell-1)},A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|U^{(\ell-1)}_{0},A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U^{(\ell)}_{0}))\right],

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
A^{(\ell)}\perp\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell-1)}|(X_{0},Y_{1},A^{(1:\ell-1)})
and the data processing inequality,
(b)
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{I}(A^{(\ell)};A^{(1:\ell-1)},X_{0}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})=0
, and
(c)
follows from the fact that
Y_{1}\perp(A^{(1:\ell-1)},X_{0})|U^{(\ell-1)}
.
∎

This result allows us to simplify our analysis since the prediction in the RHS consists of just the layer
\ell
approximation
\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}
as opposed to approximations for the first
\ell
layers
\tilde{A}^{(1:\ell)}
. The following result uses Lemma
37
to derive an upper bound for each individual term in the decomposition of Lemma
36
.

Lemma 38
.

For all
d,N,L\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, if
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the finite-width MLP process and
\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{L}
are independent random variables such that for all
Y_{t+1}\perp\tilde{A}^{(1:L)}|(A^{(1:L)},X_{t})
, then for all
\ell\in[L]
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right]\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})U^{(\ell-1)}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right).

Proof.

In the proof below, we use the notation
f_{A^{(\ell:L)}}
to denote the depth
L+1-\ell
MLP with ReLU activation units and weights parameterized by
A^{(\ell:L)}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U^{(\ell)}_{0}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U_{0}^{(\ell-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U_{0}^{(\ell-1)})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},U_{0}^{(\ell-1)})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U_{0}^{(\ell-1)})-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y_{1}-f_{\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},A^{(\ell+1:L)}}\left(U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right)^{2}\Big|A^{(\ell+1:L)},\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\left(f_{A^{(\ell:L)}}\left(U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right)-f_{\tilde{A}^{(\ell)},A^{(\ell+1:L)}}\left(U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right)\right)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\left(A^{(L)}A^{(L-1)}\ldots A^{(\ell+1)}(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(A^{(L)}A^{(L-1)}\ldots A^{(\ell+1)}(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right)^{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{=}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})U_{0}^{(\ell-1)}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right)

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
37
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
13
,
(c)
comes from the fact that for all
n
and
x,y\in\Re^{n}
,
\|x-y\|^{2}\geq\|\mathrm{ReLU}(x)-\mathrm{ReLU}(y)\|^{2}_{2}
,
(d)
follows from Jensen’s inequality, and
(e)
follows from the independence and variance assumptions of the finite-width MLP data generating process.
∎

With this result, we now derive an upper bound for the
0
-horizon distortion.

Lemma 39
.

(
0
-horizon distortion upper bound)

For all
d,N,L\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, if
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated by the finite-width MLP process and
\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{L}
are independent random variables such that for all
t
,
Y_{t+1}\perp\tilde{A}^{(1:L)}|(A^{(1:L)},X_{t})
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]\ \leq\ \sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}\right)U^{(\ell-1)}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\tilde{P}^{(\ell+1)}_{0}\|\tilde{P}^{(\ell)}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}\right)U^{(\ell-1)}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
36
and
(b)
follows from Lemma
38
.
∎

It is worth noting that the above upper bound exhibits only a linear dependence on
L
(ignoring logarithmic factors). This is an improvement over results whose analyses depend on VC dimension
[
Bartlett et al., 1998
,
Bartlett et al., 2019
]
for which the dependence on depth
L
is quadratic. This is because in worst-case analysis, an analogous error term to the logarithm of Lemma
39
may compound
exponentially
in the depth of the network. The average-case framework allows us to avoid such a penalty since this error is
in expectation
not exponential in the depth. We now present the final preliminary result which upper bounds the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function.

Theorem 40
.

(finite-width MLP
0
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound)

For all
d,N,L\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},\sigma^{2},\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the finite-width MLP process then

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)\ \leq\ \left(\frac{(L-2)N^{2}+N+dN}{2}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(e^{\frac{2\epsilon}{L}}-1\right)}\right).

Proof.

For all
\ell\in[L]
, let
\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}=A^{(\ell)}+Z^{(\ell)}
where
Z^{(\ell)}\perp A^{(\ell)}
and each element of
Z^{(\ell)}
is
\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\delta^{2})
, where
\delta^{2}=\sigma^{2}(e^{2\epsilon/L}-1)/d^{(\ell-1)}
and
d^{(\ell-1)}
denotes the dimension of
U^{(\ell-1)}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};A^{(1:L)}|\tilde{A}^{(1:L)},X_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(A^{(\ell)}-\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}\right)U^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Z^{(\ell)}U^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}N}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma^{2}N(e^{\frac{2\epsilon}{L}}-1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|U^{(\ell-1)}_{0}\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}Nd^{(\ell-1)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
39
, and
(b)
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[Z^{(\ell)\top}Z^{(\ell)}]=\frac{\sigma^{2}N(e^{2\epsilon/L}-1)}{d^{(\ell-1)}}I_{d^{(\ell-1)}}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(A^{(1:L)};\tilde{A}^{(1:L)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ \mathbb{I}(A^{(\ell)};\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\ \mathbf{h}(\tilde{A}^{(\ell)})-\mathbf{h}(\tilde{A}^{(\ell)}|A^{(\ell)})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{d^{(\ell-1)}d^{(\ell)}}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{d^{(\ell-1)}}\right)\right)-\frac{d^{(\ell-1)}d^{(\ell)}}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\delta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{d^{(\ell-1)}d^{(\ell)}}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}d^{(\ell-1)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{d^{(\ell-1)}d^{(\ell)}}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}(e^{\frac{2\epsilon}{L}}-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(L-2)N^{2}+N+dN}{2}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(e^{\frac{2\epsilon}{L}}-1\right)}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
. The result follows from the definition of the rate-distortion function.
∎

Note that the bound in Theorem
40
is only
linear
in the parameter count of the MLP. In the following subsection, we will leverage this rate-distortion upper bound and Theorem
27
to arrive at an upper bound on the error for the finite-width MLP setting.

6.4.3
Main Result

With the theoretical tools developed in the previous section, we now establish the main result, which upper bounds optimal error with data generated by the finite-width MLP process.

Theorem 41
.

(finite-width MLP error upper bound)

For all
d,N,L\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the finite-width MLP process then, for all
T
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{P}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{2LT}{\sigma^{2}P}\right)\right),

where
P=(L-2)N^{2}+N+dN
denotes the total parameter count of the network.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(A^{(1:L)})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{P\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(e^{\frac{2\epsilon}{L}}-1\right)}\right)}{2T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{P\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}\left(e^{\frac{P}{2LT}}-1\right)}\right)\right)}{2T}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}\frac{P}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{2LT}{\sigma^{2}P}\right)\right),

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
27
,
(b)
follows from Theorem
40
,
(c)
follows by setting
\epsilon=P/2T
, and
(d)
follows from the fact that for all
x\in\Re_{+}
,

\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}(e^{x}-1)}\right)\leq\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}x}\right).

∎

Theorem
41
establishes an upper bound which is only
linear
in the total parameter count of the network (
O(P)
). This notably improves upon existing results from the frequentist line of analysis
[
Bartlett et al., 1998
,
Harvey et al., 2017
]
which derive an upper bound which is
\tilde{O}(PL)
. As mentioned in the previous section, we are able to arrive at these stronger results by leveraging an expectation with respect to the prior distribution as opposed to a worst-case assumption over a confidence set.

Since we observe empirically that
deep
MLPs are able to effectively learn even in the presence of limited data, our error analysis in the Bayesian framework provides results which are closer to qualitative observations made in empirical studies. However, the results of this section are not sufficient to explain how learning may be possible when the dataset size is
smaller
than the parameter count of the model which generated the data. Such results require stronger assumptions about the
dependence
between weights in the neural network. In the following section, we explore this phenomenon in a setting in which an
infinite
width MLP generates the data, but still a learning algorithm can attain small error relatively modest amounts of data.

6.5
Infinite-Width Multi-Layer Perceptrons

Figure 6
:
We depict our infinite-width MLP data generating process above. It consists of input dimension
d
, an infinite number of hidden units with ReLU activations, and output dimension
1
. We denote the weights of the first layer via an infinite dimensional matrix
A
and the weights of the output layer by an infinite dimensional vector
\theta
. To enforce structure which enables learning, we assume an appropriate prior distribution which results in a
concentration
of the weights of
\theta
as depicted by the solid (as opposed to dotted) lines in the above diagram. We assume that the final output
Y
is the sum of the final network output and independent Gaussian noise
Z
.

In this section, we study a data generating process based on a two-layer neural network of
infinite
width. While the results we present in this section are limited to a two-layer architecture, the techniques can be generalized to treat infinite-width MLPs with any number of layers.

6.5.1
Data Generating Process

For an infinite-width MLP, we are interested in estimating a function which can be uniquely identified by an infinite-dimensional matrix
A\in\Re^{\infty\times d}
which represents the first-layer weights, and an infinite-dimensional vector
\theta\in\Re^{\infty}
of output-layer weights. In our Bayesian framework, there is a known prior distribution
\mathbb{P}((A,\theta)\in\cdot)
. The neural network has input dimension
d
and output dimension
1
. In this analysis, we restrict our attention to a particular prior distribution on the weights of the network. This prior distribution is describe by a
Dirichlet process
, which we now describe.

A Dirichlet process takes as input a
scale parameter

K
and a base distribution
P
. In our case, we
P
to be the uniform distribution
\text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
over the unit sphere in
d
dimensions. Each realization of our Dirichlet process is a probability mass functions with countable support in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Hence, each realization can be expressed as a countable subset
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\ldots\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and probabilities
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\cdots
assigned to elements of that subset. Notably, with probability one,
\bar{\theta}\geq 0
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bar{\theta}_{n}=1
.

For all
t
, let
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

Y_{t+1}=W_{t+1}+\sqrt{K}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{t}),

with

\theta_{n}=\begin{cases}\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5,\\
-\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5,\end{cases}

where
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\ldots
and
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\cdots
express an independent sample from our Dirichlet process and
W_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
is independent Gaussian noise of known variance
\sigma^{2}
.

The scale parameter
K
influences diffusiveness of
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\cdots
. As
K
decreases, the distribution becomes increasingly sparse, with probability eventually concentrating on a single component
\bar{\theta}_{n}=1
when
K=0
. As
K
increases, the distribution tends toward uniform.

In spite of the infinite width, the variance
Y_{t+1}
is finite. This property is shared by the neural tangent kernel (NTK), which also represents an infinite width neural network. However, unlike the NTK setting, with the Dirichlet prior, weights are sparse.

6.5.2
Preliminary Results

Techniques used in previous sections to bound the rate-distortion function do not extend gracefully to infinite-width MLPs. In particular, they would yield infinite, and thus vacuous, upper bounds. We now introduce an alternative technique, which builds on the following lemma, which we establish via adapting the analysis of
Barron [1993]
.

Lemma 42
.

(approximation via multinomial)

For all
K,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},
let
(A,\bar{\theta})
be drawn from the Dirichlet process with scale parameter
K
and base distribution
{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
and for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\theta_{n}=\begin{cases}\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5\\
-\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5\\
\end{cases}.

If for all
i\in[m]
,
c_{i}\overset{iid}{\sim}{\rm Categorical}(\bar{\theta})
and
\tilde{A}_{i}=A_{c_{i}}
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\underbrace{\sqrt{K}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{t})}_{\rm true\ function}-\underbrace{\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{c_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t})}_{\rm approximation}\right)^{2}\right]\ \leq\ \frac{K}{m}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{K}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{t})-\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{c_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{c_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t})\right)^{2}-\left(\sqrt{K}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{t})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{c_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{K}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t}))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{t})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{K}{m},

where
(a)
follows from the fact that the two expressions in the difference have the same expectation and
(b)
follows from the fact that the
c_{i}
’s are independent and
\mathbb{E}[{\rm sign}(\theta_{c_{i}})]=0
.
∎

Despite the fact that the MLP is of
infinite
width, we can achieve finite distortion via a simple finite-sample approximation. We now upper bound the nats of information contained in this finite-sample approximation to arrive at a rate-distortion upper bound.

Lemma 43
.

(Dirichlet-multinomial entropy bound)

For all
K,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, let
(A,\bar{\theta})
be drawn from a Dirichlet process with scale parameter
K
and base distribution
{\rm Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. If for all
i\in[m]
,
\bar{A}_{i}\overset{iid}{\sim}{\rm Categorical}(\bar{\theta})
, and
\tilde{A}_{i}=\argmin_{a\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon}}\|\bar{A}_{i}-a\|^{2}_{2}
is the closest vector in an epsilon-cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, then

\mathbb{H}(\tilde{A})\ \leq\ K\ln\left(1+\frac{m}{K}\right)\left(\ln m+d\ln\frac{3}{{\epsilon}^{2}}\right),

where
\tilde{A}=(\tilde{A}_{1},\tilde{A}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{m})
.

Proof.

As the proof of this result requires significant mathematical machinery, we refer the reader to Appendix
B
for the result.
∎

We now present one more preliminary result, which uses Lemmas
42
and
43
to upper bound the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function.

Theorem 44
.

(infinite-width MLP
0
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound)

For all
d,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\sigma^{2},\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if
(X,Y)
are generated according to the infinite-width MLP process with parameters
\theta,A
, then

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta,A)\ \leq\ K\ln\left(1+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)\ln\left(\frac{2K}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)+2dK\ln\left(1+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{4}{\epsilon}\right).

Proof.

Suppose we set
\epsilon^{\prime}=m\sigma^{2}(e^{2K/(m\sigma^{2})}-1)/2K-1\geq 0
and
m=\lceil\frac{K}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\rceil
.
For all
i\in[m]
, let
\bar{A}_{i}\overset{iid}{\sim}{\rm Categorical}(\bar{\theta})
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
denote an
\epsilon^{\prime}
-cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with respect to
\|\cdot\|^{2}_{2}
and for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, let

\tilde{A}_{i,\epsilon}=\argmin_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{\epsilon}}\ \|\bar{A}_{i}-a\|^{2}_{2}.

Let
\tilde{A}=(\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{m})
and
\tilde{\theta}=(\tilde{A},{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{1}}),\ldots,{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{m}}))
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}(Y_{1};\theta,A|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\tilde{\theta},X_{0})-\mathbf{h}(Y_{1}|\theta,A,X_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}\left(Y_{1}-\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\bigg|\tilde{\theta},\tilde{A}\right)-\mathbf{h}(W)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\left(Y_{1}-\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Y_{1}-\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\left(\sqrt{K}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{0})-\frac{\sqrt{K}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\frac{2K}{m}+\frac{2K}{m^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\mathrm{ReLU}(\bar{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{m}{\rm sign}(\theta_{\bar{A}_{i}})\cdot\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\frac{2K}{m}+\frac{2K}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathrm{ReLU}(\bar{A}^{\top}_{i}X_{0})-\mathrm{ReLU}(\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0}))^{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{\frac{2K}{m}+\frac{2K}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[(\bar{A}^{\top}_{i}X_{0}-\tilde{A}_{i}^{\top}X_{0})^{2}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{2K(1+\epsilon^{\prime})}{\sigma^{2}m}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{K}{\sigma^{2}m}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
13
and the fact that conditioning reduces differential entropy,
(b)
follows from Jensen’s inequality,
(c)
follows from Lemma
42
and the fact that for all
x,y,z\in\Re
,
(x-y)^{2}\leq 2(x-z)^{2}+2(z-y)^{2}
,
(d)
follows from the fact that for all
x,y\in\Re,\ (x-y)^{2}\geq(\mathrm{ReLU}(x)-\mathrm{ReLU}(y))^{2}
, and
(e)
follows from the fact that
\tilde{A}_{i}
is the closest element in an
\epsilon^{\prime}
cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

We now upper bound the rate of
(\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\theta,A;\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{H}(\tilde{\theta})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}K\ln\left(1+\frac{m}{K}\right)\left(\ln 2m+d\ln\frac{3}{{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}K\ln\left(1+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)\left(\ln\frac{2K}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}+2d\ln\left(\frac{\epsilon\sqrt{3}}{e^{\epsilon}-1-\epsilon}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}K\ln\left(1+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)\ln\left(\frac{2K}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)+2dK\ln\left(1+\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}\epsilon}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{4}{\epsilon}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
43
plus the factor of 2 within the logarithm due to the signs,
(b)
follows by upper bounding the quantity via
m=2K/(\sigma^{2}\epsilon)
as opposed to
\lceil K/(\sigma^{2}\epsilon)\rceil
, and
(c)
follows from the fact that for all
\epsilon\geq 0
,
\ln(\epsilon\sqrt{3}/(e^{\epsilon}-1-\epsilon))\leq\ln(1+4/\epsilon)
.
∎

6.5.3
Main Result

With the theoretical tools derived in the previous section, we now establish the following upper bound on the error of an optimal agent learning from data generated by the Dirichlet process.

Theorem 45
.

(infinite-width MLP error upper bound)

For all
d,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the finite-width MLP process, then for all
T
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{K}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)+\frac{2dK}{T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{dK}\right)\right).

Proof.

The result follows from Theorems
27
and
44
and setting
\epsilon=2dk/T
.
∎

Notably, this result is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(dK/T)
despite the fact that the Dirichlet neural network process is described by a neural network with
infinite
width. The scale parameter
K
determines the degree of concentration which occurs in the output-layer weights of the network and hence controls the difficulty of learning. This example of learning under data generated by an infinite-width MLP process further demonstrates the flexibility and ingenuity of proof techniques which are encompassed in our framework. We hope that the suite of examples provided in this section enables the reader to analyze whichever complex processes they may have in mind.

Summary

•

We abbreviate the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function
H_{\epsilon,0}
as
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}

•

(monotonicity of per-timestep error)

If for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
(X_{s},Y_{s+1})
is sampled iid from some distribution
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\theta)
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+2};\theta|H_{t+1})\ \leq\ \mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta|H_{t}).

•

(
0
-horizon distortion upper bounds
T
-horizon distortion)

If
((X_{t},Y_{t+1}):t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is iid conditioned on
\theta
and, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
Y_{t+1}\perp\tilde{\theta}|(\theta,X_{t})
then, for all
T
,

\underbrace{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]}_{T-{\rm horizon\ distortion}}\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|\tilde{P}_{0})\right]}_{0-{\rm horizon\ distortion}}.

•

We abbreviate the
0
-horizon rate-distortion function
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,0}
with
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}
.

•

(0-horizon rate-distortion error bound for iid data)

If
((X_{t},Y_{t+1}):t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is iid conditioned on
\theta
, then, for all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++},

\sup_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \min\left\{\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T},\epsilon\right\}\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon.

•

(linear regression error bounds)

For all input dimensions
d
and noise variance
\sigma^{2}
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the linear regression processes, then for all
T
,

\frac{d}{2T}W\left(\frac{2T}{d(8+\frac{d}{d-2}\sigma^{2})}\right)\ \leq\ \mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \left(\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\right)_{+}+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right),

where
W
is the Lambert W function.

•

(logistic regression error upper bound)
For all input dimensions
d
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated according to the logistic regression process, then for all
T,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{d}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{4d}\right)\right).

•

(finite-width MLP error upper bound)

For all input dimensions
d
, widths
N
, depths
L
, and noise variances
\sigma^{2}
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the finite-width MLP process, then for all
T
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{P}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{2LT}{\sigma^{2}P}\right)\right),

where
P=(L-2)N^{2}+N+dN
denotes the total parameter count of the network.

•

(infinite-width MLP error upper bound)

For all input dimensions
d
, scale parameters
K
, if for all
t
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
are generated according to the infinite-width MLP process, then for all
T
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{K}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)+\frac{2dK}{T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{dK}\right)\right).

7
Learning from Sequences

In the previous section, we focused on the special case of learning from a sequence of data which is iid when conditioned on
\theta
. However, in general, machine learning systems will have to reason about data which does not obey this rigid structure. For instance, suppose that
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
is a sequence of text tokens from a book. It’s clear that such a sequence would not be iid even when conditioned on
\theta
, as the order of the tokens plays a critical role in deriving meaning. When we relax the iid assumption, a natural question is whether the sample complexity changes. Sample complexity bounds established in the literature make assumptions about the mixing time of the data generating process
[
Mohri and Rostamizadeh, 2008
,
Kuznetsov and Mohri, 2017
,
Roy et al., 2021
,
Ziemann et al., 2024
]
. It is unclear whether these assumptions hold for practical problems. In our framework, Theorem
17
, which upper and lower bounds error via the
T
-horizon rate-distortion function, does not make any assumptions about the mixing time. In this section, we apply this theorem to general auto-regressive processes.

7.1
Data Generating Process

In this section, we will analyze learning from sequential data generated by a binary AR(K) process and by a transformer model with context length
K
. In each case, the data sequence is a random process
(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},\ldots)
and the learned model is parameterized by a random index
\theta
.

7.2
Binary AR(K) Process

7.2.1
Data Generating Process

We begin with a simple AR(K) process over a binary alphabet. Our framework can easily adapt to learning from such
sequential
data. In this problem, we are interested in estimating random vectors
\theta=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{K})\in\Re^{d\times K}
from data generated in the following way. Let
(X_{0},X_{1}\ldots,X_{K-1})\sim{\rm Unif}(\{0,1\}^{K})
. For all
t\geq K
, let

X_{t+1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ w.p. }\sigma\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}\right)\\
0&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases},

where
\sigma
denotes the sigmoid function and
\phi_{t}=\Phi_{X_{t}}
for some known vectors
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. For all
k
, we assume that each
\theta_{k}
is independent and distributed
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/K)
.

7.2.2
Preliminary Results

As in all prior examples, our strategy is to leverage rate-distortion theory to arrive at an error upper bound. We begin with the following result which upper bounds the rate-distortion function in the binary AR(K) problem setting.

Lemma 46
.

(binary AR(K)
T
-horizon rate-distortion upper bound)

For all
d,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\epsilon\in\Re_{++}
, if for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},X_{t}
is generated according to the binary AR(K) process, then for all
T
,

\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta_{1:K})\ \leq\ \frac{dK}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right).

Proof.

For
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,K-1\}
, let
\tilde{\theta}_{k}=\theta_{k}+Z_{k}
where
Z_{k}\perp\theta_{k}
and
Z_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}\cdot 8\epsilon/K)
. For all
t
, let
H_{t}
denote
(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{t})
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\tilde{P}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{I}(H_{T};\theta_{1:K}|\tilde{\theta}_{1:K})}{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(X_{t+1};\theta_{1:K}|\tilde{\theta}_{1:K},H_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(X_{t+1};\theta_{1:K}|\tilde{\theta}_{1:K},X_{t:t-K+1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta_{1:K},X_{t:t-K+1})\ \|\ \mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}_{1:K},X_{t:t-K+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta_{1:K},X_{t:t-K+1})\ \|\ \mathbb{P}(X_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta_{1:K}\leftarrow\tilde{\theta}_{1:K},X_{t:t-K+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\ln\left(\frac{1+e^{-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}{1+e^{-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}\right)}{1+e^{-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}+\frac{\ln\left(\frac{1+e^{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}{1+e^{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}\right)}{1+e^{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\theta_{k}^{\top}\phi_{t-k+1}-\tilde{\theta}^{\top}_{k}\phi_{t-k+1}\right)^{2}\right]}{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{t:t-K+1}^{\top}(\theta_{k}-\tilde{\theta}_{k})(\theta_{k}-\tilde{\theta}_{k})^{\top}\phi_{t:t-K+1}\right]}{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi^{\top}_{t-k}\phi_{t-k+1}\right]}{K}

\displaystyle=\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
33
and
(b)
follows from Lemma
32
.

We now upper bound the rate.

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\theta_{1:K};\tilde{\theta}_{1:K})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta}_{1:K})-\mathbf{h}(\tilde{\theta}_{1:K}|\theta_{1:K})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{dK}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\left(\frac{1}{K}+\frac{8\epsilon}{K}\right)\right)-\frac{dK}{2}\ln\left(2\pi e\frac{8\epsilon}{K}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{dK}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
13
. The result follows.
∎

7.2.3
Main Result

We now present the main result of this section which upper bounds the error of learning under the binary AR(K) data generating process. The result follows directly as a result of Lemma
46
.

Theorem 47
.

(binary AR(K) error upper bound)

For all
d,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},X_{t}
is generated according to the binary AR(K) process, then for all
T
,

\mathcal{L}_{T}\ \leq\ \frac{dK}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{4dK}\right)\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta_{1:K})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\frac{dK\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\right)}{2T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{dK}{2T}\left(1+\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{4dK}\right)\right),

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
17
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
46
, and
(c)
follows by setting
\epsilon=(dK)/(2T)
.
∎

An interesting aspect of this result is that both the proof techniques and final result are hardly impacted by the fact that the sequence is
