\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta)}{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{\nu^{\prime}\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu^{\prime}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\ln\frac{\sum_{\nu\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\sum_{\nu\in\Theta^{\dagger}}\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})=\nu)\cdot\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h|\theta=\nu)}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta)}{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{\nu\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})=\nu)\cdot\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h|\theta=\nu)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta)}{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\mathbb{P}(H_{T}|\theta\leftarrow\theta^{\dagger})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{\nu\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|H_{t}=h,\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})=\nu)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{\nu\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|H_{t}=h,\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})=\nu)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{\nu\in\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}}\mathbb{P}(H_{T}=h,\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\left(\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}=\nu|H_{t}=h,\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}=\nu)}+\ln\frac{\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}=\nu)}{\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})=\nu)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})\right]+{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})\in\cdot)+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot|H_{T},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\|\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot))\right]

where
(a)
follows from the log sum inequality and
(b)
follows from the fact that
\theta^{\dagger}
and
f(\theta^{\dagger})
have the same support and hence the expression can be written as a KL divergence. Further,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{P}(f(\theta^{\dagger})\in\cdot))+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot|H_{T},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\|\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot))\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))+\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};H_{T},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))+\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})+\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};H_{T}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))+\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})+\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{I}(Y_{t+1};\theta^{\dagger}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))+\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})+\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta^{\dagger},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right],

The theorem statement follows from the fact that these relations hold for all
\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}
, the definition of
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}
, and by dividing both sides of the inequality by
T
.
∎

\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})
represents error incurred in the process of learning
\theta^{\dagger}
. When
\epsilon
is optimized for each
T
, this error vanishes as
T
grows. However, the misspecification error will often remain bounded away from zero for all
T
as it represents the shortfall of making predictions using
\mathbb{Q}
instead of
\mathbb{P}
.

In the special case where
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
are equivalent measures, the misspecification error vanishes as
T
grows. The following corollary of Theorem
63
establishes this.

Corollary 64
.

(misspecified learner with matching support error bound )

For all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
are equivalent measures then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\ \big\|\ \hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)\right)}{T}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon\right).

Proof.

Let
\theta^{\dagger}\overset{a.s.}{=}\theta
. The result follows from Theorem
63
since
\theta^{\dagger}\overset{a.s.}{=}\theta
results in
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})]=0
for all
t
and
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)=\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)
.
∎

Remarks about
\theta^{\dagger}

Since the upper bound of Theorem
63
holds for all
\theta^{\dagger}
for which
\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
are equivalent measures, deriving an upper bound reduces to applying Theorem
63
with one such
\theta^{\dagger}
. The choice of
\theta^{\dagger}
may dramatically impact the resulting upper bound. One may consider an extreme case in which
\theta^{\dagger}\perp\theta
, and hence
\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})=0
. However, for this choice of
\theta^{\dagger}
, the misspecification error becomes very large, as

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\theta^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right]

is very large when
\theta^{\dagger}
is independent of
\theta
.

In the section to follow, we study two concrete examples involving misspecified linear models. These examples serve to illustrate application of Theorems
64
and
63
. Section
9.3
then demonstrates that these theorems can be used to study misspecification of much more complex models such as neural networks.

9.2
Linear Regression with Misspecification

We now study two kinds of misspecified linear models. One with a misspecified prior mean and one with a missing feature.

9.2.1
Data Generating Process

Recall the linear regression model of Section
6.2
, which is parameterized by a random vector
\theta\in\Re^{d}
of feature coefficients with prior distribution
\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}/d)
. For each
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, inputs and outputs are generated according to a random vector
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

Y_{t+1}=\theta^{\top}X_{t}+W_{t+1},

where
W_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for known variance
\sigma^{2}
.

9.2.2
Mean Misspecified Algorithm

We first study the case of a misspecified mean. In particular, we consider
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(\mu,I_{d})
. The following result upper bounds the error introduced by this misspecified prior.

Theorem 65
.

(mean misspecification error bound)

For all
d,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated by the linear regression process and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(\mu,I_{d}/d)
then

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{\|\mu\|^{2}_{2}}{2T}+\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right).

Proof.

Let
\theta^{\dagger}=\theta
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)\right)}{T}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{\|\mu\|^{2}_{2}}{2T}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{\|\mu\|^{2}_{2}}{2T}+\frac{d}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{T}{\sigma^{2}d}\right)+\frac{1}{2T}\ln\left(1+\frac{d}{T}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Corollary
64
,
(b)
follows from the formula of KL divergence between two multivariate normal distributions and the fact that
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)=\mathbb{H}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta)
, and
(c)
follows from Theorem
31
.
∎

Note that the error vanishes as
T
grows. This is intuitive, as the misspecified prior shares the support of the correctly specified prior. In the following example, we will study a case where the support of the misspecified prior is a small subset.

9.2.3
Missing Feature Misspecified Algorithm

Consider an agent that ignores the final component of
\theta
. Let
\mathbf{1}_{i}
denote the
i
th standard basis vector. To model this, let
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,(I_{d}-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top})/d)
, noting that
I_{d}-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top}
is the identity matrix with the
d
th diagonal element changed to zero. In this case,
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are
not
equivalent measures since
\mathbb{Q}(\theta_{d}=0)=1
. The following result upper bounds the error incurred by this misspecified prior.

Theorem 66
.

(missing feature error bound)

For all
d,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated by the linear regression process and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,(I_{d}-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top})/d)
then

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{d-1}{2T}\left(\ln(T)+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2d\sigma^{2}}.

Proof.

For
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,d-1\}
, let
\theta^{\dagger}_{i}=\theta_{i}
and let
\theta^{\dagger}_{d}=0
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})\right]+\frac{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))}{T}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{0}\|P^{\dagger}_{0})\right]+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta^{\top}X_{t}-\theta^{\dagger\top}X_{t}\right)^{2}\right]}{2\sigma^{2}}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta-\theta^{\dagger}\right)^{\top}\left(\theta-\theta^{\dagger}\right)\right]}{2\sigma^{2}}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2d\sigma^{2}}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
63
. We now bound the rate-distortion function.

Let
\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\in\Re^{d}
be a random vector such that for all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,d-1\}
,
\theta_{i}=\tilde{\theta}_{i}^{\dagger}+Z_{i}
, where
Z_{i}\perp\tilde{\theta}
and
Z_{1:d-1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\epsilon I_{d-1}/d)
. Meanwhile assume that
\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}_{d}=\theta_{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\theta^{\dagger},H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta,H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta^{\top}X_{t}-\theta^{\dagger\top}X_{t}\right)^{2}\right]}{2\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d-1}{2d\sigma^{2}}\epsilon.

Meanwhile we have,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})}{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left(\mathbf{h}(\theta^{\dagger})-\mathbf{h}(\theta^{\dagger}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2\pi e}{d-1}\right)-\mathbf{h}(\theta^{\dagger}-\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2\pi e}{d-1}\right)-\mathbf{h}(Z_{1:d-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2\pi e}{d-1}\right)-\frac{d-1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\epsilon 2\pi e}{d-1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{2\pi e}{d-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-1}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right).

Therefore, the result follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2d\sigma^{2}}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2d\sigma^{2}}+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{d-1}{2T}\ln\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)+\frac{d-1}{2d\sigma^{2}}\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2d\sigma^{2}}+\frac{d-1}{2T}\left(\ln\left(T\right)+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right),

where
(a)
follows from setting
\epsilon=1/T
.
∎

Note that the first term in this bound vanishes as
T
grows. The second term, on the other hand, represents irreducible error due to the fact that
\tilde{\theta}
ignores the final feature.

Our analysis can be extended to treat a prior that misspecifies the noise variance
\sigma^{2}
. And as we will see, when a feature is missing, this additional misspecification can greatly reduce error. To model this, we use model parameters to encode not only
\theta
but also
\sigma
. We take the distribution of the random variable
\sigma
to be a Dirac delta function centered at some value
\sigma_{*}\in\Re_{++}
. For the misspecified prior, we let
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,(I_{d}-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top})/d)
, as before, and let
\mathbb{Q}(\sigma\in\cdot)
be the Dirac delta function centered at
\sqrt{\frac{1}{d}+\sigma^{2}_{*}}
. The following result establishes that the error with this misspecified prior is smaller than with
\mathbb{Q}(\sigma\in\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(\sigma\in\cdot)
. In particular, the misspecification error
1/2d\sigma^{2}
is replaced by
\ln(1+1/d\sigma^{2})/2
. This represents an enormous reduction when
\sigma
is very small.

Theorem 67
.

(missing feature with inflated noise variance error bound)

For all
d,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated by the linear regression process and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)=\mathcal{N}(0,(I_{d}-\mathbf{1}_{d}\mathbf{1}_{d}^{\top})/d)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\sigma=\sqrt{1/d+\sigma^{2}_{*}})=1
then

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{d-1}{2T}\left(\ln(T)+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\theta^{\dagger}_{i}=\theta_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,d-1\}
and
\theta^{\dagger}_{d}\overset{a.s.}{=}0
. Let
\sigma^{\dagger}=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{d}}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\theta^{\dagger},\sigma\leftarrow\sigma^{\dagger},X_{0}))\right]+\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\theta^{\dagger},\sigma\leftarrow\sigma^{\dagger},X_{0}))\right]+\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[(\theta^{\top}X_{0}-\theta^{\dagger\top}X_{0})^{2}\right]}{2(\frac{1}{d}+\sigma^{2})}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[(\theta^{\top}X_{0}-\theta^{\dagger\top}X_{0})^{2}\right]}{2(\frac{1}{d}+\sigma^{2})}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d-1}{2T}\left(\ln(T)+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-1}{2T}\left(\ln(T)+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(1+\frac{1}{d\sigma^{2}}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
66

∎

9.3
Neural Scaling Laws

Theorem
63
also offers insights into trade-offs arising in the development of more complex machine learning models. One example where this result can be used to generate new insight arises in the study of neural scaling laws. In particular, how to optimally balance misspecification versus statistical errors subject to computational constraints
[
Hoffmann et al., 2022
,
Kaplan et al., 2020
]
.

Consider a simple model of computation in deep learning, where computation is measured in FLOPs, which is the product of the parameter count of the trained model and the size of the training dataset. Increasing the parameter count reduces misspecification error but at the cost of statistical error. Meanwhile, increasing the dataset size reduces statistical error. In this section, we discuss the problem studied by
Jeon and Roy [2024]
of sizing the model versus the dataset to minimize loss subject to a
fixed
FLOP count.

For a FLOP constraint
p\cdot T\leq C
, where
p
denotes the parameter count of the model and
T
the dataset size, there is a tension between
p
and
T
in minimizing the upper bound of Theorem
63
. This can be seen by first fixing a FLOP count
C
and substituting
T=C/p
. The support of the misspecified prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
includes only learning models with at most
p
parameters. If there is a constrained approximation
\theta^{\dagger}
that satisfies
\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
, as there will be for the model we formulate, then the upper bound of Theorem
63
implies

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t})\right]\ \leq\ \underbrace{\frac{p\cdot\mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};H_{T})}{C}}_{\rm statistical\ error}\ +\ \underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t}\right)\right]}_{\rm misspecification\ error}.

As our analysis will establish, the statistical error
increases
in
p
whereas the misspecification error
decreases
in
p
. Under a fixed FLOP budget, the model designer ought to select
p
to balance between the two sources of error. In the following section, we present a neural network model for which we can mathematically analyze this optimal allocation.

Our analysis will lead to a bound (Theorem
72
) on the error suffered by a finite-width neural network used to predict labels generate by an infinite width neural network. By choosing the number of parameters
p^{*}
of the finite-width neural network to minimize this error bound subject to the FLOP count constraint
p\cdot T\leq C
, we will establish a scaling law (Theorem
72
) of the form

p^{*}=\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{C}).

In other words, up to logarithmic factors, the optimal number of parameters
p^{*}
grows with the square root of the FLOP count.

9.3.1
Data Generating Process

Figure 10
:
Our Dirichlet data generating process is specified by an input dimension
d
and an infinite number of hidden units with ReLU activations. The output is scalar and passed through a sigmoidal activation unit to produce a probability from which a binary label is drawn.

We consider a data generating process which resembles the infinite-width MLP process that we analyzed in Section
6.5.1
. The MLP is uniquely identified by an infinite-dimensional matrix
A\in\Re^{\infty\times d}
, which represents the first-layer weights, and an infinite-dimensional vector
\theta\in\Re^{\infty}
of output-layer weights. The neural network has input dimension
d
and output dimension
1
. In this analysis, we restrict attention to a particular prior distribution
\mathbb{P}((A,\theta)\in\cdot)
over weights. This prior distribution is characterized by a
Dirichlet process
, which we now describe.

A Dirichlet process takes as input a
scale parameter

K
and a base distribution
P
. We take
P
to be the uniform distribution
\text{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
over the unit sphere in
d
dimensions. Each realization of our Dirichlet process is a probability mass function with countable support in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Hence, each realization can be expressed as a countable subset
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\ldots\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and probabilities
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\cdots
assigned to elements of that subset. Notably, with probability one,
\bar{\theta}\geq 0
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bar{\theta}_{n}=1
.

For all
t
, let
X_{t}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and

Y_{t+1}=\begin{cases}1&{\rm\ w.p.\ }\ \sigma\left(\sqrt{K+1}\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta_{n}\mathrm{ReLU}(A_{n}^{\top}X_{t})\right)\\
0&{\rm\ otherwise,}\end{cases}

with

\theta_{n}=\begin{cases}\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5\\
-\bar{\theta}_{n}&\text{ w.p. }0.5,\end{cases}

where
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\ldots
and
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\ldots
express an independent sample from our Dirichlet process and
\sigma
is the standard logistic function.

The scale parameter
K
influences diffusiveness of
\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\theta}_{3},\ldots
. As
K
decreases, the distribution becomes increasingly sparse, with probability eventually concentrating on a single component
\bar{\theta}_{n}=1
when
K=0
. As
K
increases, the distribution tends toward uniform.

In the next section, we consider a width-
n
approximation to this infinitely wide neural network. The number of parameters that identify this approximation scales with
p=dn
. As we will see, the data generating process exhibits two properties that are crucial for studying neural scaling laws: 1) error decreases as the dataset size
T
increases and as the parameter count
p
increases and
2)
the reduction in error is a smooth function of
p
and
T
.
Hoffmann et al. [2022]
and
Kaplan et al. [2020]
both observe a smooth and monotonic improvement in the out-of-sample log loss with increasing computational resources and data. In the following section, we will bound the error of predictions
\hat{Q}_{t}
as a function of
n
.

9.3.2
A Width-
n
Misspecified Prior Distribution

As in earlier sections, to represent misspecification we introduce an alternative probability measure
\mathbb{Q}
. In particular,
\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
is the misspecified prior distribution for our infinite-dimensional MLP. Under this misspecified prior,
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
are iid
{\rm Uniform}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, and
A_{n+1}=A_{n+2}=\cdots=0
. Further,
\overline{\theta}_{1:n}
is independently sampled
{\rm Dirichlet}(K/n)
, where
K
is the scale parameter introduced in the previous section, and
\overline{\theta}_{n+1}=\overline{\theta}_{n+2}=\cdots=0
. Finally,
\theta
is determined by
\overline{\theta}
as described in the previous section.

Note that, under our misspecified prior,
(\theta,A)
can be interpreted as the parameters of an MLP of width
n
. Furthermore, as
n
grows, the misspecified prior
\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
converges in distribution to the true prior
\mathbb{P}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
.

9.3.3
A Random Variable of Equal Distribution

To facilitate our analysis, we construct a random tuple
(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})
such that
\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\ldots)
be an iid process for which
\mathbb{P}(\delta_{j}\in\cdot)={\rm Uniform}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
. To interpret
\delta_{j}
, consider a random partition of the positive integers into
n
cells and think of
\delta_{j}
as indexing the cell to which we assign
\theta_{j}
. For all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
,
\theta^{\dagger}_{i}
aggregates weights
\theta_{j}
that are assigned to cell
i
in the following way:

\theta^{\dagger}_{i}={\rm sign}\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\theta_{j}\right)\cdot\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}|\theta_{j}|.

Further,
\theta^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\theta^{\dagger}_{n+2}=\cdots=0
. There are many ways to sample
A^{\dagger}
such that
\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
. To fix on one, for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we let
A^{\dagger}_{i}=A_{j_{i}}
, where
j_{i}=\argmax_{j:\delta_{j}=i}|\theta_{j}|
. Further,
A^{\dagger}_{n+1}=A^{\dagger}_{n+2}=\cdots=0
. It is straightforward to show that, by the aggregation property of the Dirichlet process,
\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
.

9.3.4
Preliminary Results

We first provide a result which bounds the mean squared error of our width-n approximation derived from
(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})
.

Lemma 68
.

(width-n approximation error bound)

For all
d,K,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
(\theta,A)
are drawn according to the infinite-width MLP process and
(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})
the finite-width approximation, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\dagger\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]\ \leq\ \frac{10}{n}.

Proof.

We begin by introducing the following abbreviated notation:

\phi_{i}={\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0}),\quad S_{i}={\rm Sign}(\theta_{i}),\quad S^{\dagger}_{i}={\rm sign}\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\theta_{j}\right).

Furthermore, let

\phi_{i}=\mu+\epsilon_{i},

where
\mu=\mathbb{E}[{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})]
and
\epsilon_{i}
is zero-mean.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}\phi_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\phi^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\phi_{j}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\phi^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\left(\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\phi_{j}\right)-\phi^{\dagger}_{i}\right)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\phi_{j}\right)-S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\phi^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|(\mu+\epsilon_{j})\right)-S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot(\mu+\epsilon^{\dagger}_{i})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\mu-S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\mu+\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\epsilon_{j}-S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\epsilon^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\mu^{2}\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|-S^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\epsilon_{j}-S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\epsilon^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\mu^{2}\left(1-2\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{i}^{\dagger}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|+\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)+\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right)^{2}\epsilon_{j}^{2}+\epsilon^{\dagger 2}_{i}-2S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\frac{\theta_{j_{i}}}{|\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|}\epsilon^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\mu^{2}\left(1-2\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}S_{i}^{\dagger}S_{j}\cdot\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|+\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right)^{2}\right)+\epsilon^{2}\left(\sum_{j:\delta_{j}=i}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right)^{2}+1-2S^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot\frac{\theta_{j_{i}}}{|\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|}\right)\right]

where
(a)
follows from the fact that the signs are independent and zero-mean across the partitions and Lukac’s Proportion Theorem which allows us to split out the
\mathbb{E}[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}]
and
(b)
follows from the fact that the cross terms are zero-mean.

We now bound this sum of expectations by considering the events:
E_{i}:|\theta_{j_{i}}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|\geq 0.5
for which
S^{\dagger}_{i}=S_{j_{i}}
and the complement
\lnot E_{i}:|\theta_{j_{i}}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|<0.5
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}\phi_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\phi^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\left(\mathbb{P}(E_{i})\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mu^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\left(\left(1-\left|\frac{\theta_{j_{i}}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\right)^{2}+\sum_{j\neq j_{i}:\delta_{j}=i}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{i}^{\dagger}}\right)^{2}\right)\bigg|E_{i}\right]+\mathbb{P}(\lnot E_{i})\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\left(\mathbb{P}(E_{i})\cdot 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mu^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\left(1-\left|\frac{\theta_{j_{i}}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\right)^{2}\bigg|E_{i}\right]+\mathbb{P}(\lnot E_{i})\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\left(\mathbb{P}(E_{i})\cdot 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mu^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\right)\left(1-\left|\frac{\theta_{j_{i}}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|\right)^{2}\right]+\mathbb{P}(\lnot E_{i})\cdot 4\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\left(\frac{\frac{2K}{n}}{2+\frac{K}{n}}+4\left(1-\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{K/n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{\leq}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\left(\frac{2K}{2n+K}+\frac{4K}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta^{\dagger 2}_{i}\right]\cdot\frac{5K}{n}

\displaystyle\overset{(f)}{=}\left(\frac{1+\frac{K}{n}}{K+1}\right)\cdot\frac{5K}{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10}{n}

where
(a)
follows from the fact that conditioned on the event
E_{i}
,
S^{\dagger}_{i}=S_{j_{i}}\perp S_{j}
for all
j\neq j_{i}:\delta_{j}=i
.
(b)
follows from the fact that
\sum_{j\neq j_{i}:\delta_{j}=i}(\theta_{j}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i})^{2}\leq(\sum_{j\neq j_{i}:\delta_{j}=i}|\theta_{j}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|)^{2}\leq(1-|\theta_{j_{i}}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|)^{2}
,
(c)
follows from the fact that the expected squared difference is larger when not conditioned on
E_{i}
,
(d)
follows the fact that
|\theta_{j_{i}}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|
is distributed
{\rm Beta}(1,K/n)
and that
\mu^{2}+\epsilon^{2}<1
,
(e)
follows from the fact that
(1-0.5^{K/n})\leq K/n
for
n\geq 1
, and
(f)
follows from the fact that
|\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|
is distributed
{\rm Dirichlet}(K/n)
in
n
dimensions.
∎

With this bound on the width-n error in place, we derive the following upper bound on the misspecification error.

Lemma 69
.

(misspecification error bound)

For all
d,n,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\epsilon\geq 0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\|P^{\dagger}_{0}\right)\right]\ +{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot))\ \leq\ \ \frac{10(K+1)}{n}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\|P^{\dagger}_{0}\right)\right]\ +{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta,A\in\cdot))

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{0}\|P^{\dagger}_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{K+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})-\sqrt{K+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A^{\dagger}_{i}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(K+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta^{\dagger}_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A^{\dagger}_{i}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{10(K+1)}{n},

where
(a)
follows from the aggregation property of Dirichlet process which implies
\mathbb{P}((\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}((\theta,A)\in\cdot)
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
32
, and
(c)
follows from Lemma
68
.
∎

We now provide an upper bound for the
T
-horizon rate-distortion function.

Lemma 70
.

(width-
n
approximation
T
-horizon rate-distortion bound)

For all
K,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
\theta,A
are generated by the infinite-width MLP process and
\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger}
is the width-
n
approximation then for all
\epsilon\geq 0
,

\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})\ \leq\ K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)\cdot\left(\ln\left(\frac{4Kn}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)+d\ln\left(\frac{3(K+1)}{2\epsilon}\right)\right)+K\ln(2).

Proof.

We begin by bounding the rate. Let
m=2K/((K/n+1)\epsilon)
and let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{m})
be an iid random process for which
\mathbb{P}(C_{i})={\rm Categorical}(|\theta^{\dagger}|)
. For all
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, let

\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{i}=\argmin_{a\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}}\ \|A^{\dagger}_{i}-a\|^{2}_{2},

where
\epsilon^{\prime}=2\epsilon/(K+1)
and
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
is an
\epsilon^{\prime}
-cover of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
w.r.t the squared L2 distance. For all
i
, we use
\mathcal{G}_{i}
to denote the set
\{j:\delta_{j}=i\}
. Recall that
j_{i}=\argmax_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{i}}|\theta_{j}|
. Let

\tilde{A}^{\dagger}=\left((\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{C_{1}},\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{C_{2}},\ldots,\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{C_{n}}),\ (A_{j}:\forall j{\rm\ s.t.\ }\forall i,\ j\neq j_{i})\right).

Meanwhile let

\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}=\left((C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{m}),\ (S_{C_{1}},S_{C_{2}},\ldots,S_{C_{m}}),\ (S_{j}:\forall j{\rm\ s.t.\ }\forall i,\ j\neq j_{i}),\ \left(\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|:\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},\ j\in\mathcal{G}_{i}\right)\right).

Let
\tilde{n}_{m}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}\left[\exists j\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}{\rm\ s.t.\ }C_{j}=i\right]
denote the number of unique outcomes drawn from

(C_{1:m})
. We abbreviate
S_{C_{1}},S_{C_{2}},\ldots,S_{C_{m}}
as
S_{C_{1:m}}
. Finally, recall that for all
i
,
E_{i}=|\theta_{j_{i}}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|\geq 0.5
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger};\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{I}\left(C_{1:m},\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{1:m},S_{C_{1:m}}\ ;\ \theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{I}\left((S_{j}:\forall j{\rm\ s.t.\ }\forall i,\ j\neq j_{i});\theta^{\dagger}\bigg|C_{1:m},\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{1:m},S_{C_{1:m}},\left(\left|\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{i}}\right|:\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},\ j\in\mathcal{G}_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{n}^{\dagger}\ln\left(2m\left(\frac{3}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)^{d}\right)\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\lnot E_{i})\cdot\ln(2)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}K\ln\left(1+\frac{m}{K}\right)\cdot\left(\ln(2m)+d\ln\left(\frac{3}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)\right)+n\left(1-\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\frac{K}{n}}\right)\ln(2)

\displaystyle\leq K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)\cdot\left(\ln\left(\frac{4Kn}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)+d\ln\left(\frac{3(K+1)}{2\epsilon}\right)\right)+K\ln(2),

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
(|\theta_{j}/\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|:\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},\ j\in\mathcal{G}_{i})\perp(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})
from Lukac’s proportion theorem,
(b)
follows from Theorem
5
where
(C_{1:m},\tilde{A}^{\dagger}_{1:m},S_{C_{1:m}})
are encoded as
\tilde{n}_{m}^{\dagger}
words, each with length
\ln(2m)
to denote the unique value in
(-1,-(m-1)/m,-(m-2)/m,\ldots,-1/m,1/m,\ldots,(m-1)/m,1)
and length
\ln((3/\epsilon^{\prime})^{d})
to denote the element of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
and the fact that if
E_{i}
, the conditional mutual information is
0
, and
(c)
follows from Lemma
80
.

We now bound the distortion. Let

F(x)=\sqrt{K+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}x),

and let

\tilde{F}^{\dagger}(x)=\sqrt{K+1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\theta_{j_{C_{i}}}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(\tilde{A}^{\dagger\top}_{C_{i}}x)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}\setminus\{j_{C_{i}}\}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A^{\top}_{j}x)\right).

Then,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger},X_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger},X_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger},X_{0})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger},X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\theta,A,X_{0})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger},X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|F(X_{0})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}\in\cdot|F(X_{0})\leftarrow\tilde{F}^{\dagger}(X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(F(X_{0})-\tilde{F}^{\dagger}(X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]}{8}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(F(X_{0})-\sqrt{K+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]}{4}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sqrt{K+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})-\tilde{F}^{\dagger}(X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\leq}\frac{\mathbb{V}\left(\sqrt{K+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\ \Big|\ \theta,A,\theta^{\dagger},X_{0}\right)}{4}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{m}\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime}}\|X_{0}\|_{2}\right)^{2}\right]}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{\leq}\mathbb{V}\left(\sqrt{K+1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\right)-\mathbb{V}\left(F(X_{0})\right)+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(f)}{=}(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}\right]+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(g)}{=}(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\right)^{2}+\sum_{i}^{m}\sum_{k>i}^{m}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})\right)\left(\sum_{l\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{k}}}\frac{\theta_{l}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{k}}|}{\rm ReLU}(A_{l}^{\top}X_{0})\right)\right]

\displaystyle-(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}^{2}{\rm ReLU}(A^{\top}_{j}X_{0})^{2}\right]+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(h)}{=}(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}\right)^{2}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})^{2}+\sum_{i}^{m}\sum_{k>i}^{m}\mathbb{P}(C_{i}=C_{k})\cdot\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{i}}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{m|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}\right)^{2}{\rm ReLU}(A_{j}^{\top}X_{0})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle-(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}^{2}{\rm ReLU}(A^{\top}_{j}X_{0})^{2}\right]+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}(K+1)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{|\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|}\right)^{2}+\frac{m-1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\theta^{\dagger}_{i}\right)^{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta^{\dagger}_{1}}\right)^{2}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\theta_{i}^{2}\right]+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\overset{(j)}{=}\left(K+1\right)\left(\frac{1}{m(\frac{K}{n}+1)}+\frac{m-1}{m}\cdot\frac{K+n}{n(K+1)}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{K}{n}+1}-\frac{1}{K+1}\right)+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle=\left(K+1\right)\left(\frac{1}{m(\frac{K}{n}+1)}-\frac{1}{m(K+1)}\right)+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{m\left(\frac{K}{n}+1\right)}+\frac{(K+1)\epsilon^{\prime}}{4},

\displaystyle=\epsilon,

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},\tilde{A}^{\dagger})
fully identifies
\theta,A
,
(b)
follows from Lemma
33
,
(c)
follows from Lemma
32
,
(d)
follows from the definition of conditional variance,
(e)
follows from the law of total variance,
(f)
follows from the fact that the two expressions have the same expectation,
(g)
and
(h)
follows from the fact that the cross terms have mean
0
,
(i)
follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[{\rm ReLU}(A_{i}^{\top}X_{0})^{2}]\leq 1
, and
(j)
follows from the fact that
|\theta_{j}/\theta^{\dagger}_{C_{i}}|
is distributed
{\rm DirichletProcess}(K/n,{\rm Uniform}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}))
,
|\theta^{\dagger}_{i}|
is distributed
{\rm Dirichlet}([K/n,\ldots,K/n])
in
n
dimensions, and
\theta_{i}
is distributed
{\rm DirichletProcess}(K,{\rm Uniform}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}))
.
∎

9.3.5
Main Results

Using the above results, we can now bound the error when learning with the width-
n
approximation.

Theorem 71
.

(width-
n
misspecified learner error upper bound)

For all
n,d,K,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if for all
t\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,T-1\}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated by the infinite-width MLP process and
\hat{Q}_{t}
is generated by the width-
n
approximation then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\ \leq\ \underbrace{\frac{11K}{n}}_{\rm misspecification\ error}\ +\ \underbrace{\frac{K(d+4)\ln^{2}\left(4T\right)}{T}}_{\rm statistical\ error}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger},A^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \frac{K}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)\cdot\left(\ln\left(\frac{4Kn}{\left(K+n\right)\epsilon}\right)+d\ln\left(\frac{3(K+1)}{2\epsilon}\right)\right)+\frac{K\ln(2)}{T}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}\frac{K}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{Tn}{\left(K+n\right)(K+1)}\right)\cdot\left(\ln\left(\frac{4Tn}{\left(K+n\right)}\right)+d\ln\left(\frac{3T}{2}\right)\right)+\frac{K\ln(2)}{T}+\frac{K+1}{T}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K(d+1)}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{K+1}\right)\cdot\ln\left(4T\right)+\frac{3K}{T}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K(d+4)}{T}\ln\left(1+\frac{T}{K}\right)\cdot\ln\left(4T\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K(d+4)}{T}\ln^{2}\left(4T\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
70
and
(b)
follows by setting
\epsilon=(K+1)/T
. The result follows from Theorems
63
and Lemma
69
.
∎

By sizing the finite-width approximation to minimize the preceding error bound, we obtain a scaling law.

Theorem 72
.

(compute-optimal parameter count)

For all
d,n,K,T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and FLOP counts
C\geq 5000
, if for all
t\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
,
(X_{t},Y_{t+1})
is generated by the infinite-width MLP process,
\hat{Q}_{t}
is generated by the width-
n
approximation, and if
n^{*}
minimizes the upper bound of Theorem
71
subject to
d\cdot n\cdot T\leq C
then

d\cdot n^{*}=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{C}\right).

Proof.

By Theorem
71
we are looking for
n^{*}
where

n^{*}=\argmin_{n\in\left[\frac{C}{d}\right]}\frac{11K}{n}+\frac{K(d+4)\ln^{2}(4t)}{t};\ \text{ s.t. }n\cdot d\cdot t\leq C.

(1)

For all
C\geq 5000
, the loss of
n\geq 4C/(de^{3})
is suboptimal. For instance, if we let
n=\sqrt{C}/(d\ln(C))
and if
C\geq 5000
then for all
d
,

\displaystyle\frac{d\cdot 11\cdot\ln(C)}{\sqrt{C}}+\frac{d+4\cdot\ln^{2}\left(4\ln(C)\cdot\sqrt{C}\right)}{\sqrt{C}\cdot\ln(C)}\leq\frac{11de^{3}}{4C}+\frac{d(d+4)\cdot 36}{de^{3}}.

Since the expression in equation
1
is concave up with a unique minimizer, we know that
n^{*}<4C/de^{3}
. Then,

\displaystyle n^{*}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{n\in\left[\frac{4C}{de^{3}}\right]}\frac{11K}{n}+\frac{K(d+4)\ln^{2}(4t)}{t};\ \text{ s.t. }n\cdot d\cdot t\leq C

\displaystyle=\argmin_{n\in\left[\frac{4C}{de^{3}}\right]}\frac{11}{n}+\frac{(d+4)\ln^{2}(4t)}{t};\ \text{ s.t. }n\cdot d\cdot t\leq C

\displaystyle=\argmin_{n\in\left[\frac{4C}{de^{3}}\right]}\frac{11}{n}+\frac{nd(d+4)\ln^{2}(\frac{4C}{nd})}{C}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}n\text{ s.t. }\frac{11}{n^{2}}=\left(\frac{d(d+4)\ln^{2}\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)}{C}-\frac{2d(d+4)\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)}{C}\right)

\displaystyle=n\text{ s.t. }C=\frac{n^{2}d(d+4)\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)}{11}\left(\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)-2\right),

where
(a)
follows from
1
st order optimality conditions

Due to monotonicity, we can derive upper and lower bounds for the value of
n
via lower and upper bounds of the above RHS respectively. We begin with the upper bound for
n^{*}
:

\displaystyle n^{*}

\displaystyle=n\leq\frac{4C}{de^{3}}\ \text{ s.t. }C=\frac{n^{2}d(d+4)\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)}{11}\left(\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)-2\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}n\text{ s.t. }C=\frac{d^{2}n^{2}}{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{11C}}{d}.

where
(a)
follows from the fact that for
n>4C/(de^{3})
,
d^{2}n^{2}/11
lower bounds the above expression. We now derive the lower bound for
n^{*}
:

\displaystyle n^{*}

\displaystyle=n\text{ s.t. }C=\frac{n^{2}d(d+4)\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)}{11}\left(\ln\left(\frac{4C}{nd}\right)-2\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}n\text{ s.t. }C=n^{2}d^{2}\ln^{2}(4C)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\sqrt{C}}{d}\right).

where
(a)
follows from the fact that
d^{2}n^{2}\ln^{2}(4C)
upper bounds the above expression. The result follows.
∎

9.3.6
Discussion

Our theoretical analysis of neural scaling laws was inspired by extensive empirical analysis of transformer-based language models
[
Kaplan et al., 2020
,
Hoffmann et al., 2022
]
. Those two empirical studies posited differing functional forms – both flawed – to map training dataset size and learning model parameter count to out-of-sample loss. The function proposed by
Kaplan et al. [2020]
overlooks an irreducible error term.
Hoffmann et al. [2022]
corrected this and carried out a regression analysis to conclude that the optimal parameter count ought to grow
linearly
in the optimal dataset size.

Theorem
72
establishes that the optimal parameter count is
d\cdot n^{*}=\tilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{C})
. This asymptotic bound corroborates the insight of
Hoffmann et al. [2022]
that, up to logarithmic factors, the optimal parameter count grows
linearly
in the optimal dataset size. In spite of this congruence, our theoretical error bound differs significantly from the functional form posited by
Hoffmann et al. [2022]
. We detail some of the differences now. The empirical results of
Hoffmann et al. [2022]
suggest that the error decays as

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{T}\|\hat{Q}_{T})\right]\ \approx\ \frac{B}{p^{0.34}}+\frac{A}{T^{0.28}},

(2)

where
A
and
B
are constants. This result is derived from empirical analysis of transformer models trained via stochastic gradient descent. Meanwhile, the results of this section hold for the Bayesian posterior predictive with respect to a misspecified prior. A natural question is: what is the gap between the error attained by current optimization algorithms and what is achievable based on information theory?

Theorem
63
provides an interesting answer to this question. Let
\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)=\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
and assume that the data pairs
(X_{0},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{T},Y_{T+1})
are iid when conditioned on
\theta
. Then, Theorem
63
becomes:

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{T}\|\hat{Q}_{T})\right]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P_{T}^{*}\ \|\ P^{\dagger}_{T}\right)\right]+\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right).

The first term is independent of
T
and mirrors the first term in Equation
2
; error which diminishes as
p
increases. Meanwhile, we expect the second term, which represents statistical error, to be
\tilde{O}(1/T)
(as in Theorem
71
). On the other hand, the empirical rate expressed by Equation
2
suggests that, for stochastic gradient descent, statistical error decays at a rate of only
T^{0.28}
. Developing algorithms that close this gap offers a promising direction for future research.

Summary

•

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be an alternative probability measure on
(\Omega,\mathbb{F})
, which may assign a misspecified prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)\neq\mathbb{P}(\theta\in\cdot)
but with the correct conditional data distribution
\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\theta)=\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\theta)
.

•

The
t
th prediction generated based on the misspecified prior is
\hat{Q}_{t}=\mathbb{Q}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|H_{t}\leftarrow H_{t})
.

•

For all
\theta^{\dagger}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)
and
\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot)
are equivalent measures, let

\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})=\inf_{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\in\tilde{\Theta}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}}\ \mathbb{I}(\theta^{\dagger};\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}),

where

\tilde{\Theta}_{\epsilon,T}=\left\{\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger}\in\tilde{\Theta}:\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta^{\dagger},\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t})\|\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta}^{\dagger},H_{t}))\right]\leq\epsilon\right\}.

•

(misspecified learner error bound)
For all
T\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}\left(P^{*}_{t}\|\hat{Q}_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\theta^{\dagger}\in\Theta^{\dagger}}\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P^{*}_{t}\|P^{\dagger}_{t})\right]+\frac{{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(\theta^{\dagger}\in\cdot)\|\mathbb{Q}(\theta\in\cdot))}{T}}_{\rm misspecification\ error}\ +\ \underbrace{\inf_{\epsilon\geq 0}\ \left(\frac{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}_{\epsilon,T}(\theta^{\dagger})}{T}+\epsilon\right)}_{\rm statistical\ error}\right),

where
P^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t+1}\in\cdot|\theta\leftarrow\theta^{\dagger},H_{t})
.

10
Conclusion

Summary.
This monograph provided a rigorous mathematical framework for analysis of achievable performance in machine learning. By adopting a Bayesian framing, we were able to leverage connections to Shannon’s information theory. In doing so, we elucidated the intimate connection between learning and optimal lossy compression via rate-distortion theory, providing a streamlined process for deriving error bounds for a plethora of settings ranging from simple to complex. Regardless of whether the data is iid or exhibits sequential or hierarchical structure, our general results apply and provide accurate insights. We also extended our framework and results to the study of misspecified models.

Future Research.
Our framework leaves open many directions of future research. Except for the case of linear regression, we established only rate-distortion upper bounds, leaving lower bounds as a topic for future research. It would be interesting to understand, for example, the extent to which tools such as the Donsker-Varadhan lower bound can be leveraged to derive general rate-distortion lower bounds. Beyond that, our framework and results may be leveraged to understand puzzling new phenomena as they continue to emerge in the field of machine learning.

Appendix

Appendix A
Lower Bounds for Linear Regression

We now introduce a lemma relating expected KL divergence to mean-squared error, a distortion measure that is prevalent in the literature. This relation will allow us to derive a lower bound for the rate-distortion function in the Gaussian linear regression setting.

Lemma 73
.

For all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\sigma^{2}\geq 0
, if
\theta:\Omega\mapsto\Re^{d}
has iid components that are each
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian and symmetric,
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, and
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta^{\top}X,\sigma^{2})
, then for all proxies
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}
,
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
4\nu^{2}\|X\|^{2}_{2}+\sigma^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
X
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X])}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda\left(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]\right)}\big|X\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]\right)}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda\left((\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}])^{\top}X\right)}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda(\theta^{\top}X)}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda(\theta^{\top}X)}|\tilde{\theta},X\right]\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[e^{-\lambda(\theta^{\top}X)}|\tilde{\theta},X]^{2}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[e^{-2\lambda(\theta^{\top}X)}|\tilde{\theta},X]\big|X\right]

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-2\lambda(\theta^{\top}X)}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}e^{2\lambda^{2}\nu^{2}\|X\|^{2}_{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\left(\sigma^{2}+4\nu^{2}\|X\|^{2}_{2}\right)}{2}},

where
(a)
follows from
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]=W
which is independent from
\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
,
(b)
follows from the fact that
\theta^{\top}X
is symmetric conditioned on
X
and Jensen’s inequality,
(c)
follows from the fact that
e^{\theta^{\top}X}=\mathbb{E}[e^{\theta^{\top}X}|\theta,\tilde{\theta},X]
, and
(d)
follows from the fact that the components of
\theta
are
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian.
∎

Lemma 74
.

If
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditional on
X
(uniformly), then for all
\alpha>1
,
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
\alpha\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditional on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
.

Proof.

Assume that for some
\alpha>1
, there exists an event
\mathcal{S}
s.t.
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})>0
and
\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S}
implies that
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is not
\alpha\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X])}\big|X\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X])}|\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S},X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{>}e^{\ln\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})}\cdot e^{\frac{\alpha\lambda^{2}\nu^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\left(\alpha\nu^{2}+\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})\right)}{2}}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\left(\nu^{2}+(\alpha-1)\nu^{2}+\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})\right)}{2}},

where
(a)
holds for all
\lambda
s.t.
|\lambda|\geq|\lambda^{*}|
for some
\lambda^{*}
. Such
\lambda^{*}
exists because of the fact that
\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S}
implies that
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is not
\alpha\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
. As a result, for
\lambda
such that
\lambda^{2}>\max\left\{\frac{2\ln\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}\in\mathcal{S})}{(1-\alpha)\nu^{2}},\lambda_{*}^{2}\right\}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda(Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X])}\big|X\right]>e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu^{2}}{2}},

which is a contradiction since
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditional on
X
. Therefore the assumption that there exists
\alpha>1
and
\tilde{\theta}
s.t.
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is not
\alpha\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditional on
(X,\tilde{\theta})
cannot be true. The result follows.
∎

Lemma 75
.

If
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]\right)^{2}}{2\nu^{2}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,X)\|\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X))\right].

Proof.

We begin by stating the Donsker-Varadhan variational form of the KL-divergence. For all probability distributions
P(\cdot)
and
Q(\cdot)
over
\Re
such that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
, for densities
dP,dQ
w.r.t the Lebesgue measure,

{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(P(\cdot)\|Q(\cdot))=\sup_{g:\Re\rightarrow\Re}\left(\int_{y\in\Re}g(y)dP(y)-\ln\int_{y\in\Re}e^{g(y)}dQ(y)\right),

where the supremum is taken over measurable functions for which
\int_{y\in\Re}g(y)dP(y)
is well-defined and the expression on the right
\int_{y\in\Re}e^{g(y)}dQ(y)
is finite.

Let
P(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,X_{t}),Q(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X),
and
Z=Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
. Then, for arbitrary
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, applying the variational form of KL-divergence with
g(Y)=\lambda Z
gives us

\displaystyle{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,X)\|\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X))

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,\tilde{\theta},X)\|\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X))

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\mathbb{E}\left[Z|\theta,\tilde{\theta},X\right]-\ln\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda Z}|\tilde{\theta},X\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]\right)-\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu^{2}}{2},

where
(a)
follows from
Y\perp\tilde{\theta}|(\theta,X)
and
(b)
follows from
Z
being
\nu^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
. Since the above holds for arbitrary
\lambda
, maximizing the RHS w.r.t
\lambda
gives us:

{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,X)\|\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X))\geq\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}[Y|\theta,X]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]\right)^{2}}{2\nu^{2}}.

The result follows from taking an expectation on both sides.
∎

We establish a lower bound by first finding a suitable lower bound for the distortion function. For subgaussian random vectors, the following lemma allows us to lower bound the expected KL-divergence distortion by a multiple of the mean squared error.

Lemma 76
.

For all
\tilde{\theta}\in\tilde{\Theta}
,
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
and
\sigma^{2}\in\Re_{++}
, if
\theta:\Omega\mapsto\Re^{d}
consists of iid components each of which are
1/d
-subgaussian and symmetric,
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, and if
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta^{\top}X,\sigma^{2})
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|X\|^{2}_{2}}{2(4\|X\|_{2}^{2}+d\sigma^{2})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{I}(Y;\theta|\tilde{\theta},X).

Proof.

\theta^{\top}X
is
\|X\|_{2}^{2}/d
-subgaussian conditioned on
X
and by Lemma
73
,
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
4\|X\|_{2}^{2}/d+\sigma^{2}
-subgaussian conditioned on
\|X\|_{2}^{2}
. Lemma
74
then states that
Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\tilde{\theta},X]
is
\alpha(4\|X\|_{2}^{2}/d+\sigma^{2})
-subgaussian conditioned on
(\tilde{\theta},X)
for all
\alpha>1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{d}}_{\rm KL}(\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\theta,X)\|\mathbb{P}(Y\in\cdot|\tilde{\theta},X))\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\lim_{\alpha\downarrow 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left((\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}])^{\top}X\right)^{2}}{2\alpha\left(\frac{4\|X\|_{2}^{2}}{d}+\sigma^{2}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left((\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}])^{\top}X\right)^{2}}{2\left(\frac{4\|X\|_{2}^{2}}{d}+\sigma^{2}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\left((\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}])^{\top}X\right)^{2}}{2\left(\frac{4\|X\|_{2}^{2}}{d}+\sigma^{2}\right)}\Bigg|\|X\|^{2}_{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\frac{\|X\|^{2}_{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\|(\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]}{2\left(\frac{4\|X\|_{2}^{2}}{d}+\sigma^{2}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|X\|^{2}_{2}}{2(4\|X\|_{2}^{2}+d\sigma^{2})}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\|\theta-\mathbb{E}[\theta|\tilde{\theta}]\|^{2}_{2}\right]

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
75
and
(b)
follows from the fact that
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
∎

Appendix B
Theoretical Results for Dirichlet-Multinomial

B.1
General Combinatorics Results

We begin with the following general combinatorics results that we will eventually apply to bound the estimation error of the finite
N
Dirichlet-Multinomial process.

Lemma 77
.

For all
m,j,n,K,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
s.t.
j<n
, if

C_{m}(j)=\underbrace{\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\cdot\sum_{i=j+1}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\cdot\ldots\cdot\sum_{i>j+m-1}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}}_{m},

then

\frac{1}{m!}\frac{K^{m}}{N^{m}}\ln^{m}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+m-1+j}\right)\leq C_{m}(j)\leq\frac{1}{m!}\frac{K^{m}}{N^{m}}\ln^{m}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1+j}\right).

Proof.

We first prove the upper bound via induction. Base Case:
n=1

\displaystyle C_{1}(j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{N}\int_{j-1}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1+j}\right)

Assume inductive hypothesis is true for
m=k
.

\displaystyle C_{k+1}(j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\cdot C_{k}(i+1)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K}{N}\int_{j-1}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}\cdot C_{k}(x+1)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{j-1}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}\cdot\ln^{k}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+x}\right)dx

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\cdot\ln^{k+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+x}\right)\bigg|^{n}_{j-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\ln^{k+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1+j}\right).

We now prove the lower bound also via induction. Base Case:
m=1

\displaystyle C_{1}(j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{N}\int_{j}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(\frac{K+n}{K+j}\right)

Assume inductive hypothesis is true for
m=k
.

\displaystyle C_{k+1}(j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\cdot C_{k}(i+1)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K}{N}\int_{j}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}\cdot C_{k}(x+1)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{j}^{n}\frac{1}{K+x}\cdot\ln^{k}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+k+x}\right)dx

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\cdot\ln^{k+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+k+x}\right)\bigg|^{n}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\ln^{k+1}\left(\frac{K+k+n}{K+k+j}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K^{k+1}}{N^{k+1}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\ln^{k+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+k+j}\right).

The result follows.
∎

Lemma 78
.

For all
i,n,K,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
2\leq K\leq\sqrt{N}
and
n\leq N
, then

\frac{1}{(2i)!}\frac{K^{2i}}{N^{2i}}\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)-\frac{1}{(2i+1)!}\frac{K^{2i+1}}{N^{2i+1}}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+2i}\right)\geq 0.

Proof.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}1-\frac{1}{2i+1}\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)-\frac{1}{2i+1}\frac{K}{N}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)-\frac{1}{2i+1}\frac{K}{N}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+2i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2i)!}\frac{K^{2i}}{N^{2i}}\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)-\frac{1}{(2i+1)!}\frac{K^{2i+1}}{N^{2i+1}}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+2i}\right)

where
(a)
follows for all
n\leq\left(K-1\right)e^{\frac{3N}{K}}-K
which is implied by
n\leq N
for
K\geq 2
.
∎

Lemma 79
.

For all
n,K,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
2\leq K\leq\sqrt{N}
and
n\leq N
, then

\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\right)\geq 1-\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}1-\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\frac{1}{(2i+1)!}\frac{K^{2i+1}}{N^{2i+1}}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+r}{K+2i}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\rceil}\frac{1}{(2i)!}\frac{K^{2i}}{N^{2i}}\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+r}{K-1}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}1-\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor}\frac{1}{(2i)!}\frac{K^{2i}}{N^{2i}}\ln^{2i}\left(\frac{K+n}{K-1}\right)-\frac{1}{(2i+1)!}\frac{K^{2i+1}}{N^{2i+1}}\ln^{2i+1}\left(\frac{K+n}{K+2i}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\geq}1-\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right),

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
77
, and
(b)
follows from Lemma
78

∎

B.2
Lemmas pertaining to Dirichlet Multinomial

The following result upper bounds the expected number of unique classes drawn from a Dirichlet-multinomial distribution with
n
draws and
\alpha=[K/N,\ldots,K/N]\in\Re^{N}
.

Lemma 80
.

For all
n,K,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
s.t.
K\leq\sqrt{N}
and
n\leq N
, if
\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}\sim{\rm DirMult}(n,\alpha)
for
\alpha=\left[K/N,\ldots,K/N\right]\in\Re^{N}
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{i}>0]}\right]\leq K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{i}>0]}\right]

\displaystyle=N\cdot\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{i}>0)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}N\cdot\left(1-\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{1}=0,\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{2}+\cdots+\tilde{\theta}^{\prime}_{N}=n)\right)

\displaystyle=N\cdot\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(K)\Gamma(n+1)}{\Gamma(n+K)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{K}{N}\right)\Gamma\left(1\right)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{K}{N}(N-1)\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{K}{N}(N-1)\right)\Gamma\left(n+1\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=N\cdot\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(K)}{\Gamma(n+K)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(n+K-\frac{K}{N}\right)}{\Gamma\left(K-\frac{K}{N}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=N\cdot\left(1-\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(K-\frac{K}{N}+i\right)}{\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}K+i}\right)

\displaystyle=N\cdot\left(1-\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(1-\frac{\frac{K}{N}}{K+i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}N\cdot\left(1-\left(1-\frac{K}{N}\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right).

where
(a)
follows from the aggregation property of the Dirichlet-multinomial distribution and
(b)
follows from Lemma
79
.
∎

Note that this upper bound is
independent
of
N
. In the next section, we will apply the dominated convergence theorem to bound the number of unique basis functions drawn by our learning model.

The extension to a Dirichlet Process
(N\to\infty)
follows trivially.

Lemma 81
.

For all
n,K\in\mathbb{Z}_{++}
, if
\theta
is distributed according to a Dirichlet process with base distribution
{\rm Uniform}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
with scale parameter
K
and
\tilde{\theta}\sim{\rm Multinomial}(\theta)
, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}>0]}\right]\leq K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}>0]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}>0]}\bigg|\mathcal{W}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{w\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}^{\prime}>0]}\bigg|\mathcal{W}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{w\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}^{\prime}>0]}\bigg|\mathcal{W}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{\leq}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right)

\displaystyle=K\ln\left(1+\frac{n}{K}\right),

where in
(a)
,
\mathcal{W}_{N}
is a subset of the first
N
elements of
\mathcal{W}
and
\tilde{X}_{N}
is
{\rm DirMult}(n,\alpha_{N})
where
\alpha_{N}=[K/N,\ldots,K/N]\in\Re^{N}
and
\mathcal{W}_{N}
is the set of classes,
(b)
follows from the dominated convergence theorem since
|\sum_{w\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}}\mathbbm{1}_{[\tilde{\theta}_{w}^{\prime}>0]}|\leq n
, and
(c)
follows from Lemma
80
.
∎

See
43

Proof.

Let
N_{m}
denote the number of
unique
classes from the
m
independent categorical samples
\tilde{A}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{A}_{m}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}(\tilde{A})

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{m}\cdot\left(\ln(m)+\ln(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon}|)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\leq}K\ln\left(1+\frac{m}{K}\right)\left(\ln m+d\ln\frac{3}{{\epsilon}^{2}}\right)

where
(a)
follows from Theorem
5
and the fact that a realization
\tilde{A}
can be mapped to a codeword of length
N_{m}(\ln(2m)+\ln(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon}|))
by using
\ln(m)
nats to encode the number of times a class was drawn and
\ln(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon}|)
nats to encode the class and
(b)
follows from Lemma
81
and the fact that
|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\epsilon}|\leq(3/{\epsilon}^{2})^{d}
.
∎
